+H. Computation Time and Control Interval
+Figure 9 shows inference time per tick for the laptop. Inference time of the GCN-LSTM is 2.0 ms (median) and 4.1 ms (99th percentile), while the inference time of the LSTM-only model is 1.5 ms (99th percentile). In the routing experiments, the forecaster is called at every 1-millisecond tick, but the simulator does not wait for the forecaster to finish. Hence, the GCN-LSTM model is slower than the simulated clock on this CPU because the median inference time exceeds the duration of one tick. It is the tick and not the horizon that is the right basis of comparison here.
+
+Fig. 9. Median (p50) and 99th-percentile (p99) inference time per tick for the GCN-LSTM and LSTM-only forecasters relative to the 5 ms forecast horizon (laptop CPU, two threads).
+Inference is also not the limiting factor here. As measured on the cloud machine using the original controller, one tick of the guarded controller requires 3.4 ms in median and 69 ms in 99% percentile when driven by the score, and 2.7 ms in median and 82 ms in 99% percentile when driven by the GCN-LSTM, while the GCN-LSTM takes 2.9 ms on average in median on the same cloud machine; in the case of the mixed family, one tick takes 44 - 51 ms in median. A breakdown of the costs of running the guarded controller with GCN-LSTM on the mixed network revealed that almost all the costs are due to computing weighted shortest paths in NetworkX. The controller, therefore, is the bottleneck of the control loop and a deployment solution requires either the computation of paths off-line or some hardware support.
+To see whether coarser-grained forecasting maintains the routing result, guarded GNN was tested again, but in this run, the forecaster is called only every two or five ticks and its prediction is usable only after its interval, thus, allowing 2 or 5 ms per invocation for the GCN-LSTM to perform inference. This changes the reduction in total drops of the controlled runs on unseen seeds from 4.14% (in the case of the original guarded controller, calling the GCN-LSTM every tick and with the same training seed) to 4.33% and 4.48%, with the number of route changes going down to 53 and 36 compared with 68. Thus, when predicting the future every five ticks, which even allows time enough for the 99% percentile GCN-LSTM inference on the laptop computer (taking 4.1 ms), the routing result stays preserved, and in this coarseness the GCN-LSTM inference stays in line with the simulated clock. Still, the complete duration of the controller tick (2.7 - 3.4 ms at the median, 44 - 51 ms within the mixed family) is out of reach.
+I. Comparison with the State of the Art
+Load-balancing solutions relying on switches (CONGA, HULA, and Clove) and those implemented on the hosts (FlowBender, Hermes) are not reproduced in the simulation because flowlets, ECN marking, and transport protocol behaviors are lacking; a FlowBender-style emulation with ECN marking at a queue threshold and rehashing after consecutive congested ticks is evaluated below. Thus, the quantitative part of this comparison will be based on baseline schemes capturing major design ideas: static hashing (ECMP), reactive threshold rerouting, unguarded forecast-driven rerouting, and model-free guarded rerouting. The model-free guarded controller follows the reservation rule set by Hedera and also demonstrates the dampened and conditional movements associated with FlowBender and Hermes; however, it is not a replacement of those systems in terms of its functioning process.
+FlowBender-style baseline. Since the required state exists in the simulator, a FlowBender-style baseline [20] was implemented: a link marks ECN when its queue exceeds 10% of its buffer, and a flow whose path contains a marking link for two consecutive ticks is rehashed to a randomly chosen different equal-cost path; there is no capacity check, budget or risk signal. On the benchmark seeds (Table V) it reduces total drops by 10.62%, 4.90 points more than guarded score (95% CI of the difference [+2.25, +8.09]), and has the lowest delivery delay of all strategies (mean 7.21 ms, 99th percentile 13.58 ms), but it makes 1,039 route changes per run and reorders 9.4% of the delivered packets in 42% of the flows; a more aggressive setting (5% threshold, one tick) gives 10.79% with 2,159 route changes. In the closed-loop model its mean FCT is 3.4% longer than that of guarded score without a reordering penalty ([+1.8%, +5.3%]) and 12.4% longer with it ([+9.8%, +14.5%]; Table A6). FlowBender-style rehashing therefore behaves like the score-driven controller without rules: it removes more drops and lowers open-loop delay than the guard, at the cost of much more churn and reordering, which lengthens flow completion in the closed-loop model.
+NeuralFabric is distinct from reactive approaches of Table I in its ability to rely on forecasts and is different from learning solutions like RouteNet and T-GCN that are assessed through their ability to predict or optimize routes rather than perform online rerouting. Our results show that, under the conditions of a millisecond time scale, the guarded controller does not show any loss in performance using the current score instead of predictions; whether the same holds for CONGA, FlowBender, and Hermes was not tested.
+J. Discussion
+Key results. Temporal neural networks were shown to predict the future state of link congestions better than persistence in overall AP in all the settings used, including a pilot experiment evaluating workload families not seen in the training set and without any structural repeating cases of stride and LLM-training. There is no gain from use of Graph Convolutional Network (GCN). Prediction of new congestion appears to be hard and predictions suffer from the upward bias of temperature-scaled probabilities. Forecast-driven rerouting does not beat current congestion-dependent rerouting with identical admission rules in all single-rule ablation experiments and in presence of delays of up to five telemetry or actuation steps. All guarded schemes deliver a 2.9 - 3.4% gain in the number of packets delivered per tick, reduce delay and drop totals by 4.9 - 5.7% while making less reordering actions than aggressive rerouting, despite the fact that most of total drops are incurred on the host links and are beyond control by any rerouting mechanism. They are also shown to decrease flow completion time in our closed loop model setting, unlike reactive and unguarded GNN rerouting, which increases completion times; against the score-driven controller without rules, the guard shortens completion times only when reordering is penalised.
+Interpretation. The guarded rerouting procedure is not the best policy for drop avoidance: removing all admission rules from the model-free controller doubles its drop reduction, but this policy changes 62 - 74 times as many routes and loses most of the guard's delay advantage over ECMP. Thus, the admission rules define the tradeoff point between drops, churn, and delay; the removal of capacity check alone becomes the major alteration in this respect; nevertheless, gradual integration of the rules reveals their redundancy with each other (Section V-E). Considering that the five-tick horizon is small relative to the lifetime of the flows and primary control loop lacks of delay, the contribution of the risk signal becomes marginal in case of adoption of the capacity check, since stochastic risk signal works as well as the existing one on drops, though with 3.5 times as many route changes, and a perfect passive five-tick congestion label brings no improvement over it (V-F). The congestion measurements inside the check itself also add no detectable benefit: a check on the demand placed on each link by the flows' sending rates and paths, without queue and arrival terms, gives a drop reduction that is not detectably different (Section V-E). What matters in this setting is information about where the flows are and what they send, rather than measured queues or arrivals, used as a gate on where they may move; congestion information used as a trigger for which flows move does not change drops. On the other hand, without admission rules, risk signal gains importance and increases in forecast error is the factor increasing the number of moves. Under such circumstances, forecast-based routing becomes 4.9 percentage points worse than score-based routing.
+Implications. With respect to a millisecond-scale control loop equipped with an admission control policy, the priority should lie in improving the admission control policy rather than on improving the forecaster itself. Forecast-driven routing should be compared with a model-free signal that drives the same action, and each admission rule should be ablated. Improvements in prediction may otherwise be incorrectly identified as an improvement in admission control policies. These considerations depend on the particular experimental setting, and thus generalization of these findings is not possible to control loops where delays are longer with respect to the forecasted horizon, to wider fabrics, or to traffic with a longer lifespan. The delay sweep does not indicate any benefit of forecasting at delays of up to five ticks; with five ticks of actuation delay, the forecaster did worse by 0.72 points. 
+Limitations. Packets-batches simulator operates with 1 ms tick rate and does not consider ECN, PFC, and RDMA. The closed-loop configuration used in simulations for flow completion times is a coarser, loss-based model as opposed to the real transport protocol. The telemetry in question is the ideal case in the main set of experiments; the span of the delay sweep consists of constant delays up to five ticks. Traffic is synthetic; the traffic stride and LLM training families have repeated host pairs' usage within each seed while the former is based on the pattern of collective communication rather than on traces replication. Only one topology (k = 4) and one forecast horizon (five ticks) were investigated; the forecasting data set consists of six cases, holdout experiments were performed using one seed only, and the forecaster employs only per-link aggregated features. Some additional experiments use a single seed of GNN training. The capacity check is given exact flow rates in the main experiments; noisy, rate-blind and telemetry-estimated rates were tested (Section V-F), but not a deployed demand estimator; the finding that the queue and arrival terms add nothing (Section V-E) rests on exact knowledge of each flow's path and sending rate.
+Recommendations. Larger fabric spatial graph models (for instance, k = 8) should be validated on longer forecasting horizons with per-flow demand and interlink congestion propagation where neighboring information has more relevance. In order to produce forecasts valuable beyond state information, onset-oriented training objectives and adjusted probabilities would become necessary. Forecast-based control could be reconsidered with real transport protocols, where control loop delays would be estimated on hardware with forecasting horizons matching the delays.
+VI. Conclusion and Future Scope
+NeuralFabric examines forecast-driven rerouting in a controlled setting in which a telemetry-driven congestion forecaster and model-free signals drive the same capacity-guarded controller in a full-duplex Fat-Tree simulation. The learned forecasters ranked future congestion better than persistence in overall AP in every test scenario (on average over training seeds), though not in onset AP, but a per-link logistic regression obtained most of this gain, the spatial graph component gave no measurable advantage, onsets remained hard to predict and the probabilities were biased upwards. Forecast-driven guarded routing performed no better than the same controller driven by the current congestion score, on the benchmark and on unseen traffic, under every single-rule ablation and with up to five ticks of telemetry or actuation delay or at lighter offered loads. All guarded approaches increased delivered packets per tick by 2.9 - 3.4% and reduced pooled total drops by 4.9 - 5.7% (fabric-link drops by 20 - 25%), with about ten times fewer route changes and 41 - 71 times less packet reordering than aggressive rerouting, with lower delay than ECMP and, in a closed-loop transport model, with about 4% shorter mean flow completion times; against the score-driven controller without rules, which removes the most drops, this advantage appears only when reordering is penalised. The ablations show that removing the capacity check alone changes the outcome most, that the rules are largely redundant with one another, and that the rules as a whole trade part of the attainable drop reduction for far fewer route changes and lower delay. The main point is that, in this idealised millisecond-scale setting, with the admission rules in place, the guarded controller acts as a demand-aware reshuffler whose benefit comes from the capacity check that gates where flows may move rather than from congestion information in the signal that selects which flows move; removing the queue and arrival measurements from the check changed total drops by at most 0.26 points of the ECMP drops on average, with no detectable effect, so the check works through the demand that the flows place on each link: a random risk signal did as well as the current score on drops, though with 3.5 times as many route changes, and a perfect passive five-tick congestion label added nothing, whereas without the rules the signal matters; whether forecasting pays off in slower control loops, at longer horizons or in larger fabrics remains open.
+Future work will test larger topologies (k=8) and longer forecast horizons matched to measured control-loop delays, use realistic flow sizes and transport protocols with lossless flow control, give the forecaster per-flow demand inputs, design training objectives for congestion onsets, and implement the controller with precomputed candidate paths on programmable data planes.
+Data and Code Availability
+The simulator, training and evaluation code, checkpoints of the trained models, the raw results of every run (including the additional experiments), and the scripts that generate the figures and tables are available from the authors upon reasonable request. All datasets and traffic are generated with fixed random seeds and can be regenerated exactly with the generation scripts; the simulator is deterministic.

+NeuralFabric: A Controlled Evaluation of Forecast-Driven and Model-Free Capacity-Guarded Rerouting in Datacenter Fabrics
+Divyank Seervi, Krish, Choppa Madhu Dhanush Teja, Srishti Agarwal, Ganesh Khekare*
+School of Computer Science and Engineering,Vellore Institute of Technology, Vellore, Tamil Nadu, India
+divyank.seervi2024@vitstudent.ac.in, krish.2024@vitstudent.ac.in, choppa.madhu2024@vitstudent.ac.in, srishti.agarwal2024@vitstudent.ac.in, ganesh.khekare@vit.ac.in[*]
+
+
+Abstract -- In datacenter fabrics, hash-based multipath routing ignores load, and reactive rerouting acts only after queues have built up. This paper asks whether forecasting link congestion milliseconds ahead improves rerouting. In a packet-level simulator of a k=4 Fat-Tree with 1-ms ticks and six synthetic workload families, a graph convolutional network combined with an LSTM (GCN-LSTM) predicts the congestion of each link five ticks ahead, and forecasts or model-free signals drive the same capacity-guarded controller. The forecasters rank future congestion better than persistence (AP 0.947 against 0.880), but the graph layers add nothing and onsets remain hard to predict. Guarded rerouting raises delivered packets per tick by 2.9 - 3.4% over ECMP and lowers delay, yet reduces pooled total drops by only 4.9 - 5.7%, as host-link drops rise. A FlowBender-style queue-threshold rehash beats it on drops, throughput and 99th-percentile delay; the guard's case rests on 18 - 27 times fewer route changes, 25 - 36 times less reordering and shorter mean flow completion in a closed-loop model. Forecast-driven routing never beats the current congestion score, even on unseen traffic. The admission rules are largely redundant and trade more than half of the attainable drop reduction for 62 - 74 times fewer route changes. With the rules, a random risk signal matches the current score on drops, with 3.5 times the route changes, and a perfect passive five-tick congestion label adds nothing: the gain comes from the capacity check on where flows may move, which needs only flow rates and paths, not from congestion information in the signal; without the rules, the signal matters. The controller exceeds the 1-ms tick.
+Index Terms -- Datacenter networks, predictive routing, congestion forecasting, admission control, in-band network telemetry, graph neural networks, load balancing, Fat-Tree topology
+I. Introduction
+A. Background
+Datacenter networks interconnect thousands of servers which run distributed storage, analytics and Machine Learning (ML) workloads. Measurement studies of production datacenters show that traffic is application-dependent and spiky rather than a smooth aggregate stream [1]. To provide high bisection bandwidth with commodity switches, modern fabrics are built as multi-rooted trees such as the Fat-Tree [2], and related designs like PortLand, VL2, BCube and Jellyfish show that topology and path diversity determine how much routing freedom a controller has [3] - [6].
+The standard way to use this path diversity is Equal-Cost Multi-Path (ECMP) forwarding, which hashes every flow onto one of the shortest paths [7]. ECMP is simple and stable, but it is insensitive to load: two large flows can be hashed onto the same link while parallel links remain idle.
+B. Motivation
+When a link becomes congested, its queue grows, packets are dropped or delayed and transport protocols back off. Every millisecond of queue build-up therefore translates into lost throughput and higher tail latency for the applications sharing that link. If congestion on a link can be anticipated a few milliseconds before it materialises, flows could be moved to another equal-cost path in advance. This idea, called predictive or proactive routing, has become more feasible with the availability of fine-grained In-band Network Telemetry (INT) and fast learning-based predictors.
+C. Existing Approaches and Their Limitations
+Dynamic flow scheduling systems such as Hedera and DevoFlow move large flows using global visibility of the network state [8], [9], and centralised optimisers such as ElasticTree adapt the active fabric to demand [10]. Distributed congestion-aware load balancers such as CONGA, HULA, DRILL, Presto and LetFlow react to measured path or queue conditions at flow, flowlet or packet granularity [11] - [15]. These systems are effective, but they are reactive by construction: they respond after congestion has already been observed.
+In parallel, ML models for networking have advanced quickly. Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) such as RouteNet model the relationship between topology, routing and performance [16], and spatio-temporal graph models forecast traffic in transportation and communication networks [17] - [19]. However, an accurate forecast does not automatically yield a good routing policy. When a forecast flags a link as risky, a controller without admission rules can move many affected flows at once, which generates excessive route churn and can overload the alternative paths.
+D. Research Gap
+Two questions are frequently conflated in prediction-driven networking: whether a model can forecast congestion, and whether acting on that forecast improves system-level behaviour. Existing studies seldom evaluate the second question against strong, model-free baselines that use the same action mechanism, and they rarely report whether a spatial graph component adds value over purely temporal modelling. Without such ablations, routing gains may be attributed to the predictor when they are in fact produced by the action policy.
+E. Proposed Solution and Novelty
+This paper presents NeuralFabric, an experimental framework for a controlled evaluation of forecast-driven rerouting. A telemetry-driven congestion forecaster and several model-free signals drive one and the same capacity-guarded rerouting controller, which admits a route change only when the candidate path lowers the signalled risk, keeps path stretch bounded, respects a per-flow cooldown and a per-tick change budget, and passes a capacity check in which accepted moves sequentially reserve headroom. None of these admission rules is new on its own: sequential reservation follows the Global First Fit heuristic of Hedera [8], and the cooldown and conditional moves resemble the damping of FlowBender [20] and the cautious rerouting of Hermes [21]. The contribution of this paper is therefore not a new controller but (i) an evaluation design that keeps the action mechanism fixed while the signal changes, removes each admission rule in turn and repeats the comparison under telemetry and actuation delays, and (ii) the empirical finding this design produces: in the tested setting, the learned forecaster ranks future congestion better than persistence, yet routing on its forecasts brings no measurable benefit over routing on the current congestion score, and, with the admission rules in place, the rules rather than the signal determine the routing outcome: the guarded controller acts as a demand-aware reshuffler in the manner of Hedera: its benefit comes from the capacity check that gates where flows may move, not from congestion information in the signal that selects which flows move, and a check whose demand estimate uses only the flows' sending rates and paths, without the queue and arrival measurements, gives a drop reduction that is not detectably different (Section V-E).
+F. Contributions
+The main contributions of this paper include:
+ 1) A full-duplex, causal, packet-conserving simulator of a k=4 Fat-Tree fabric with hash-based ECMP, explicit per-direction queues, attribution of packet drops to host links and fabric links, configurable telemetry and actuation delays and an optional closed-loop transport model for flow completion times.
+ 2) A spatio-temporal Graph Convolutional Network (GCN) and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) forecaster of per-link congestion, evaluated against persistence, an LSTM-only model and logistic regression, with temperature-scaled probabilities, a calibration check, validation-tuned thresholds and onset-only metrics.
+ 3) A capacity-guarded rerouting controller that combines known admission rules (sequential capacity reservation, per-flow cooldown, per-tick change budget, path-stretch bound and minimum risk improvement) and can be driven by any per-link risk signal.
+ 4) A controlled evaluation methodology, comprising guard-only baselines that share the action mechanism, leave-one-rule-out ablations and parameter sweeps on unseen traffic, a telemetry- and actuation-delay sweep, equivalence tests, family-clustered bootstrap intervals and flow-level metrics, which separates forecast quality from routing benefit.
+G. Paper Organisation
+Section II critically reviews related work and establishes the research gap. Section III describes the simulator, dataset, forecasting models and guarded controller with their mathematical formulation. Section IV reports the experimental setup, the evaluation metrics and the additional robustness experiments. Section V presents the results, ablations, sensitivity and delay analyses and the discussion. Section VI concludes the paper and outlines future scope.
+II. Literature Review
+A. Datacenter Topologies and Multipath Routing
+The Fat-Tree architecture showed that commodity switches arranged as a multi-rooted tree can deliver full bisection bandwidth [2]. PortLand added a scalable, fault-tolerant layer-2 fabric, VL2 exposed uniform high capacity through Valiant load balancing over a Clos network, BCube proposed a server-centric modular design and Jellyfish studied random regular graphs as an alternative to hierarchical layouts [3] - [6]. These works establish that path multiplicity is the resource a load balancer exploits. ECMP remains the default way of using it because it is stateless and deterministic [7], but hash collisions between long-lived flows are a well-known weakness that motivates load-aware alternatives.
+B. Flow Scheduling and Congestion-Aware Load Balancing
+Hedera schedules large flows centrally to improve aggregate throughput over ECMP, and DevoFlow analyses the control-plane cost of obtaining the flow visibility that such scheduling requires [8], [9]. Hedera's Global First Fit heuristic already reserves the demand of each placed flow along its path, so that later placements see only the remaining capacity [8]; the sequential capacity reservation used in this paper follows the same principle. ElasticTree demonstrates demand-driven reconfiguration of the active network [10]. Among distributed schemes, CONGA uses in-network congestion feedback with flowlet switching [11], HULA implements telemetry-driven path selection in programmable data planes [12], DRILL performs micro load balancing using local queue information [13], Presto balances load at the network edge while handling packet reordering [14], LetFlow exploits flowlets for resilience under asymmetry [15], and Clove shifts flowlets between paths from the hypervisor virtual switch using ECN feedback [22]. FlowBender moves a flow to a new ECMP path only after its fraction of ECN-marked acknowledgements has stayed above a threshold for consecutive round trips, a damping rule that plays the same role as the cooldown in our controller [20]. Hermes senses path conditions with round-trip times and ECN and reroutes cautiously: a flow is moved only when it has already sent enough data, is sending below a rate cap and a clearly better path exists [21]. ConWeave makes mid-flow rerouting of RDMA traffic safe by masking packet reordering inside the network [23]. Centralised arbitration in Fastpass and priority-based transport in pFabric show that path selection, scheduling and transport interact strongly [24], [25]. These mechanisms act on observed state at sub-millisecond to millisecond time scales. The admission rules used in this paper (capacity reservation, damping and conditional moves) therefore have clear precedents, and the open question is not whether such rules work but whether a forecast adds value when they are in place.
+C. Congestion Control and In-Band Telemetry
+Data Center TCP (DCTCP), a datacenter variant of the Transmission Control Protocol (TCP), uses Explicit Congestion Notification (ECN) to keep queues short [26]. DCQCN extends ECN-based control to Remote Direct Memory Access (RDMA) deployments [27], TIMELY uses round-trip time as a congestion signal [28], and HPCC uses precise in-band telemetry to obtain link load information for rate control [29]. PINT reduces the overhead of in-band telemetry while preserving its usefulness for congestion control and path tracing [30]. These works motivate per-hop queue and utilisation measurements as forecasting inputs, but they act on sending rates rather than on path admission.
+D. Graph and Temporal Learning for Network Prediction
+Graph convolutional networks propagate information along graph edges [31], LSTM networks capture temporal dependence in sequences [32], and surveys categorise convolutional, recurrent and spatio-temporal graph models as distinct families [33]. In traffic forecasting, DCRNN combines diffusion convolution with recurrent units [17], STGCN combines graph and temporal convolutions [18], Graph WaveNet learns adaptive spatial dependencies [34] and T-GCN couples graph convolution with gated recurrent units [19]. A recent survey reviews deep learning specifically for network traffic prediction and highlights the difficulty of generalising across traffic conditions [35]. In network modelling, RouteNet and RouteNet-Fermi predict delay, jitter and loss from topology, routing and traffic [16], [36], and RouteNet-TGNN extends this line to temporal forecasting in dynamic networks [37]. These models establish that graph-temporal forecasting is feasible, but they mainly report prediction accuracy rather than the consequences of acting on predictions in an online control loop.
+E. Learning-Based Routing and Control
+Learning to Route studies deep reinforcement learning for traffic engineering and highlights how difficult it is to replace algorithmic routing logic with learned policies [38]. AuTO scales deep reinforcement learning to datacenter traffic optimisation by taking fast local decisions for short flows at the end hosts and central decisions for long flows [39]. A hybrid Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) and Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) model has been integrated with Software-Defined Networking (SDN) for access control and traffic engineering, with the GAN generating synthetic traffic [40]. This work relates to ours in two ways, through its reliance on synthetic traffic and through its coupling of a learned temporal model with SDN control, but its evaluation compares learned models with one another rather than with a model-free controller that takes the same actions, which is the comparison the present study adds. GraphCC combines graph learning with multi-agent reinforcement learning to tune ECN parameters in datacenters [41], and NTP-INT uses traffic prediction to plan telemetry paths on high-load switches [42]. For Artificial Intelligence (AI) clusters, Hopper performs congestion-aware path selection for RDMA traffic [43], and FLB shows that fine-grained load balancing interacts non-trivially with lossless flow control [44]. Acting on forecasts is also established in traffic engineering. MicroTE exploits the short-term, partial predictability of datacenter traffic matrices to route the predictable part of the traffic [45], COPE optimises routing for the traffic matrices predicted from history while bounding the penalty for unpredicted ones [46], LSTM-based traffic-matrix predictors have been proposed for SDN [47], and DOTE shows that learning traffic-engineering decisions directly from historical demands can outperform the classic predict-then-optimise pipeline in wide-area networks [48]. These systems act on traffic matrices at time scales of a second or longer; the present study asks the analogous question at the millisecond scale of per-link queue build-up, where the current state is a strong signal.

+Table I compares representative studies in terms of method, data or setting, key finding, strength and limitation with respect to forecast-driven rerouting.

+NTP-INT [42]
+Traffic prediction for telemetry planning
+High-load switches
+Prediction-driven telemetry paths
+Prediction feeds control
+Different control variable
+Hopper [43]
+Congestion-aware path selection
+AI-cluster RDMA simulation and testbed
+Path switching for RDMA traffic
+Addresses AI workloads
+Not a forecast-vs-guard study
+FlowBender [20]
+ECN-triggered flow rehashing
+Testbed and simulation
+Lower latency than ECMP with simple end-host changes
+Damps reroutes over consecutive RTTs
+Reacts to measured state only
+Hermes [21]
+Cautious, sensing-based rerouting
+Testbed and simulation
+Resilient load balancing under uncertainty
+Conditional, damped moves
+No forecasting; rules not compared with forecasts
+MicroTE [45]
+Traffic engineering on predictable traffic
+Datacenter traces and simulation
+Short-term predictability can be exploited
+Acts on predictions
+Second-scale traffic matrices, not per-link queues
+DOTE [48]
+Direct optimisation from demand history
+WAN topologies and traces
+Can outperform predict-then-optimise
+Questions explicit forecasting
+WAN, minute-scale
+
+G. Review Analysis and Research Gap
+Three observations emerge from the review. First, deployed load balancers are predominantly reactive and act at sub-millisecond to millisecond time scales, whereas the systems that act on predictions operate on traffic matrices at time scales of seconds or more. Second, learning-based network models are mostly evaluated on prediction metrics, so it remains unclear whether millisecond-scale forecasts improve routing once acted upon. Third, the admission rules that make rerouting safe are well established, but when learned forecasts drive rerouting, the results are seldom compared with model-free signals that drive the same rules, the contribution of each rule is seldom isolated, and the value of the spatial graph component is seldom measured. Consequently, the following questions remain open: (i) does a telemetry-driven forecaster rank future link congestion better than persistence on unseen workloads; (ii) does the spatial graph component add value over temporal modelling; (iii) does forecast-driven rerouting outperform the same capacity-guarded controller driven by model-free signals; and (iv) which admission rules produce the routing benefit, and does the answer survive telemetry and actuation delays? NeuralFabric is designed to answer these questions under a controlled, reproducible setting.
+III. Methodology
+A. System Overview
+Fig. 1 shows the end-to-end framework. Traffic generators produce flows on a Fat-Tree fabric; the simulator advances packets causally; a telemetry collector converts each tick into switch and link features; a forecaster or a model-free baseline produces per-link risk signals; and the capacity-guarded controller decides whether any flow should change path. Route changes affect future traffic, closing the loop.
+
+Fig. 1. NeuralFabric framework. Telemetry from the simulator feeds a per-link congestion forecaster or a model-free baseline signal; the resulting strategy-specific link-risk signals ρe drive a capacity-guarded rerouting controller, and admitted route changes influence subsequent traffic.
+B. Network and Simulator Model
+The fabric is modelled as a graph G=V,ℰ with switches and hosts as vertices and physical links as edges. A k=4 Fat-Tree, i.e. one built from 4-port switches, contains 4 core, 8 aggregation and 8 edge switches, 16 hosts and 48 physical links, of which 32 interconnect switches [2]. Each physical link {u,v} is full-duplex and is represented by two directed links u,v and v,u, each with capacity C packets per tick and buffer B packets. Time advances in ticks of 1 ms.
+The tick is the unit of time of the simulator and is taken to be 1 ms: with 1,500-byte packets, C=500 packets per tick corresponds to 6 Gb/s. Propagation delay (0.5 us) and serialisation delay (about 1.2 us per packet at 10 Gb/s) are three orders of magnitude shorter than a tick and are not modelled separately; a packet advances one hop per tick. All delays and horizons in this paper are therefore counted in ticks of 1 ms. The buffer of B = 2,000 packets (3 MB at 1,500 bytes) holds four ticks of line-rate service, so that queue build-up remains visible over several ticks of the forecast horizon before packets are dropped. This is deeper than the per-port share of typical shallow-buffered datacenter switches; smaller buffers would turn queue build-up into drops sooner and shorten the warning time available to any forecaster.
+For a directed link l at tick t, let alt be arriving packets and qlt the queue occupancy. Accepted packets, drops, served packets and the queue update are
+alt=min{alt, B−qlt}, dlt=alt−alt,
+(1)
+
+slt=min{qlt+alt, C},
+(2)
+
+qlt+1=qlt+alt−slt.
+(3)
+Packets advance at most one link per tick, so forwarding is causal. Over a run, the simulator asserts packet conservation,
+Poffered=Pdropped+Pdelivered+Poutstanding,
+(4)
+and attributes every drop to a host link (a link with a host endpoint), a fabric link (switch-to-switch) or an unroutable flow, so that Dtotal=Dhost+Dfabric+Dunroutable.
+Baseline forwarding uses ECMP. For a flow f from s to d with equal-cost shortest-path set Πsd, the path index is computed with the 256-bit Secure Hash Algorithm (SHA256) as
+if=SHA256s ∥ d ∥ idf mod Πsd,
+(5)
+which spreads flows across all core switches while keeping each flow on a single path.
+C. Dataset Generation
+No public dataset provides per-link, per-millisecond telemetry for a datacenter fabric together with the ability to replay routing decisions. A synthetic dataset was therefore generated with six traffic families, summarised in Table II. Each scenario lasts T=150 ticks. Thirty scenarios (five per family, distinct random seeds) form the forecasting dataset, and a separate set of traffic seeds is used for routing evaluation.
+TABLE IISynthetic traffic families used for data generation.
+Family
+Description
+Uniform
+Background flows between random host pairs at moderate rates
+Hotspot
+Incast: several sources send at high rate to one destination, plus background
+Elephant/mice
+Mixture of long, high-rate flows and short, low-rate flows
+Stride
+Pod-to-pod permutation that stresses the core layer
+LLM-training
+Large Language Model (LLM) training-inspired collective bursts (ring and all-to-all phases); not a trace replay
+Mixed
+Superposition of the above generators
+With a window of W=10 ticks and horizon H=5 ticks, each scenario yields T−W−H+1=136 windows, each containing all 32 fabric links. The stratified split assigns three scenarios per family to training, one to validation and one to testing (18/6/6 scenarios, i.e. 2,448/816/816 windows). Splitting by whole scenarios prevents overlapping windows from crossing partitions.
+The stride and LLM-training generators place flows on the same host pairs in every seed (stride: the same sixteen flows; LLM-training: the same ring and pod-leader pairs), so their test scenarios are structurally close to their training scenarios. Section V-B quantifies this overlap and reports results without these two families.
+D. Preprocessing and Feature Representation
+For each switch v, the node feature vector is
+xvt=qvB, ∑sCdegv, min​1,∑dC, ∑q∑B,
+(6)
+computed over the egress links of v (average queue, throughput, drops and buffer usage). For each physical fabric link e={u,v}, directional quantities are merged by taking the maximum over both directions, and the edge feature vector is
+xet=min1,a/C,  q/B,  min1,d/C,  s/C,
+(7)
+i.e. offered utilisation, normalised queue, normalised drops and served utilisation. All features lie in 0,1. The features emulate per-hop in-band telemetry but are read directly from the simulator state.
+The congestion score of link e combines served load and queue build-up,
+cet=clip0,1​max​{setC, τcqetϕB},
+(8)
+with τc=0.85 and ϕ=0.1, and the binary label is yet=1cet>τc. A link is therefore labelled congested when it serves more than 85% of capacity or holds more than 10% of its buffer. The learning target for a window ending at tick t is yet+H.
+An onset is a link-window for which yet=0 but yet+H=1; onsets are the cases where forecasting can add information beyond the current state.
+E. Forecasting Models
+1) GCN-LSTM: At each tick in the window, a two-layer GCN encodes node features [31]:
+Hl+1=ReLU​LN(D−1/2AD−1/2HlWl),
+(9)
+where A is the switch adjacency matrix with self-loops, D its degree matrix, LN layer normalisation, ReLU the rectified linear unit and H0=Xt. For link e={u,v} the per-tick input to the temporal model is
+met=Wphut ∥ hvt ∥ xet+bp.
+(10)
+An LSTM [32] processes the sequence met−W+1,...,met:
+it;ft;ot=σWmt+Uht−1+b,
+(11)
+
+gt=tanhWgmt+Ught−1+bg,
+(12)
+
+μt=ft⊙μt−1+it⊙gt, ht=ot⊙tanhμt.
+(13)
+Here gt is the candidate memory and μt the memory (cell) state; they are written differently from the congestion score cet of (8) to avoid ambiguity. A two-layer perceptron maps the final hidden state to a logit ze, and the temperature-scaled probability is
+pe=σ​ze/Ts,
+(14)
+where Ts is a temperature fitted on validation data [49]. The GCN-LSTM has 17,985 trainable parameters.
+2) Baselines: Four baselines use the same inputs and split. Binary persistence predicts yet+H=yet. Continuous persistence uses the current score cet of (8) as the prediction score. LSTM-only removes the GCN and feeds the edge features xe directly into the same LSTM and output head, which isolates the contribution of the spatial component. Logistic regression fits one model per link on the flattened Wx4 window.
+F. Training and Calibration
+Models are trained with weighted binary cross-entropy,
+ℒ=−1Nn​w+ ynlogpn+1−ynlog1−pn,
+(15)
+with positive-class weight w+=3 to counter class imbalance. After training, the temperature is obtained by minimising the negative log-likelihood over the validation windows Sval,
+Ts⋆=argminTs>0 −n∈Sval​logPyn∣σzn/Ts,
+(16)
+and the decision threshold is selected on validation data as the value that maximises the F1 score defined in (23):
+τ⋆=argmaxτ F1{1pn>τ}n∈Sval.
+(17)
+Temperature scaling is monotonic, so ranking metrics are unchanged; it rescales the confidence of the probabilities but cannot remove a constant shift such as the one introduced by the positive-class weight. Section V-A therefore measures the remaining miscalibration directly.
+G. Capacity-Guarded Rerouting Controller
+Let ρe be the link-risk signal of the chosen strategy; for forecaster-driven strategies, ρe is the temperature-scaled probability pe of (14). The risk of a path PI is
+rPI=maxe∈PIρe.
+(18)
+An active flow f on path PIf is considered only if rPIf>τm⋆, where τm⋆ is the alert threshold of strategy m. Every threshold is set on validation data: for each forecaster it is the threshold of (17) (0.75 - 0.85 for the GCN-LSTM and 0.74 - 0.78 for the LSTM-only model across training seeds); for the continuous score it is the threshold that maximises the validation F1 score of cet as a predictor of yet+H, which gives τ⋆=0.851 and thus essentially the label threshold τc=0.85; and for the binary label it is 0.5. Up to three candidate paths are generated as weighted simple paths with link cost 1+10 ρe. A candidate PI' is admissible only if all of the following hold:
+rPIf−rPI'>=δ,
+(19)
+
+PI'<=PIf+2,
+(20)
+
+De+rf+QeΔt<=α Ce ∀e∈PI'∖PIf,
+(21)
+where rf is the flow rate, De the estimated demand on directed link e (the larger of the summed active-flow rates and the observed arrivals), Qe its queue and α the headroom factor. All terms of (21) are in packets per tick: De, rf and Ce are rates, and the queue enters as the rate Qe/Δt needed to drain it within one control interval Δt=1 tick, so the check asks whether the link could carry the moved flow and clear its backlog in the next tick. Section V-E removes the queue term and the observed arrivals from (21) to test which of its terms matter. In addition, a flow may not be moved within κ ticks of its previous move, at most M moves are admitted per tick, and predictions older than one tick are rejected. Once a move is accepted, demand is reserved immediately:
+De<--De−rf ∀e∈PIf,De<--De+rf ∀e∈PI',
+(22)
+so later decisions in the same tick cannot claim the same headroom; this is the reservation principle of Hedera's Global First Fit [8], and the cooldown is a damping rule in the spirit of FlowBender [20]. Flows are processed in decreasing order of current-path risk. Algorithm 1 summarises the procedure. The default parameters are δ=0.15, α=0.90, κ=5 and M=4. They were set by hand when the controller was first implemented, before any of the experiments reported here, and were not tuned on the benchmark traffic (seeds 3000 - 3009); Section V-E varies each of them on unseen traffic.
+Algorithm 1 Capacity-guarded rerouting (one tick)
+Require: strategy-specific link-risk signals ρ, threshold τm⋆, active flows F, link states
+1:	Initialise demand De for every directed link; n<--0
+2:	for f∈F sorted by decreasing rPIf do
+3:	if n>=M then
+4:	break
+5:	end if
+6:	if rPIf<=τm⋆ or f in cooldown then
+7:	continue
+8:	end if
+9:	for PI' in up to 3 weighted simple paths of f do
+10:	if (19), (20) and (21) hold then
+11:	Reserve demand by (22); PIf<--PI'; n<--n+1
+12:	Start cooldown of f; break
+13:	end if
+14:	end for
+15:	end for
+The same controller is driven by different signals ρe to form the routing strategies: forecaster probabilities (guarded GNN, guarded LSTM), the binary persistence label (guarded persistence) and the continuous score ce (guarded score). For the binary label, path risks are 0 or 1, so (19) holds for any 0<δ<=1 exactly when the current path contains a congested link and the candidate contains none; δ has no effect on guarded persistence, which is therefore an all-or-nothing version of the same rule. Reactive rerouting moves every flow whose current path crosses a fabric link with utilisation above 0.9 to the shortest path in which each such link costs 100 and every other link 1; it has no per-tick budget or capacity check, and a flow is not moved again within five ticks. Unguarded GNN rerouting moves flows away from links whose predicted probability exceeds a fixed alert threshold of 0.7 and chooses the shortest path under the same risk-weighted link costs plus a penalty on flagged links; it keeps the five-tick per-flow cooldown but has no capacity check, per-tick budget, path-stretch bound or minimum-improvement condition. Because the guarded and unguarded GNN strategies differ in several respects at once, the contribution of each rule is isolated separately by the leave-one-rule-out ablation of Section V-E. Score, no rules (Tables V and IX) denotes the score-driven guarded controller with every admission rule except the alert threshold removed.
+H. Evaluation Protocol
+Forecasting is evaluated on the held-out test scenarios only. Routing strategies are compared on identical traffic: for each family and traffic seed, every strategy receives exactly the same flows, which yields paired comparisons. Differences are summarised with paired bootstrap confidence intervals over (family, seed) pairs for routing and over test scenarios for forecasting. Because seeds within a family are not independent (all stride seeds share the same sixteen flows), routing intervals are also computed with a two-stage bootstrap that resamples families and then seeds within each family. A claim of "no difference" between two strategies is tested as equivalence with two one-sided tests (TOST) [50] at a margin of +-1% of the mean ECMP total drops, i.e. one fifth of the effect of the guard itself: equivalence is declared when the 90% confidence interval of the difference lies inside the margin. This margin is wide, since the whole spread among the guarded strategies (0.86 points) fits inside it, so the one-sided bounds on the difference are the main evidence; and because a percentile bootstrap over only six (or four) families undercovers, per-family differences are also reported (Table VII). All ablations, sweeps and delay experiments use the unseen traffic seeds 4000 - 4009, which were used neither for training nor for the main benchmark.
+IV. Experimental Setup
+A. Environment
+The experiments were performed on a notebook computer HP 14s-dq2xxx (CPU only without GPU; an 11th generation Intel Core i5-1135G7 2.40 GHz, 4 core 8 threads CPU with integrated Intel Iris Xe Graphics) with 8 GB of RAM running Windows 11 Home (build 26200). The software environment includes Python 3.14.3, PyTorch 2.14.1 (CPU build), PyTorch Geometric 2.8.0, NetworkX 3.7, NumPy 2.5.3 and scikit-learn 1.9.1. The PyTorch and Python versions are specified according to what is recorded by the benchmarking scripts' results file, and other package versions correspond to the project's requirements.txt (frozen). Inference time was measured with two CPU threads. Calibration and overlap analyses and experiments on out-of-scope traffic, admission policies, delays, impact on flow level and computational performance (sections V-A-V-H) were conducted on a cloud virtual machine (CPU Intel Xeon, 2.8 GHz, 2 virtual CPUs with Python 3.13 and the same PyTorch version) using the checkpoints trained on the notebook. Prior to that 156 runs of the routing benchmark across all strategies on this machine perfectly reproduced stored values. Hence, the outputs from Table IV (except its ECE column), Figure 2, the pilot experiment from Section V-B, the main benchmark (Tables V and VII; seed-benchmark rows of Table VI; Figures 4, 5, and 7), and the rows from Table VIII corresponding to "change-budget" along with the time spent on inference in Figure 9 were computed from the notebook. On the other hand, the ECEs, Figure 3, the overlap analysis from Section V-B, the "unseen-seed" rows from Table VI, Figures 6 and 8, the rest of the rows of Table VIII, the "Score, no rules" rows from Tables V and IX, Table IX, and the outcomes discussed in Sections V-E to V-H were computed from the cloud machine.
+B. Hyperparameters and Training Procedure
+The Table III below gives the parameters and configuration used in the experiments. The Adam optimizer is applied with a learning rate of 0.001, weight decay of 0.0001, batch size of 32, and gradient clipping threshold of 1.0. Training is carried out over a maximum of 50 epochs, with early stopping being activated in case of no increase in the validation AP in 10 successive epochs. Besides, the learning rate is reduced by half whenever there is no improvement in the validation AP in 5 successive epochs. The GCN-LSTM and LSTM-only models are trained on three different random seeds. In the routing scenario, the performance measures are obtained by averaging the output of the three variants of the trained models.

+C. Evaluation Metrics
+Congestion is a minority class (positive rate 0.233 overall and 0.035 among onset candidates), so accuracy alone would be misleading. Precision is P=TPTP+FP and recall is R=TPTP+FN, where True Positives (TP), False Positives (FP) and False Negatives (FN) are counted at the decision threshold. The F1 score, Average Precision (AP) and Brier score are computed as
+F1=2PRP+R,
+(23)
+
+AP=k​Rk−Rk−1 Pk,
+(24)
+
+Brier=1Nn​pn−yn2.
+(25)
+The main criterion for ranking the models is AP, which evaluates the precision - recall curve independently of the decision threshold. The area under the Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) curve (AUC) is also reported, and the Brier score evaluates the accuracy of the predicted probabilities. The forecasting metrics are calculated over all test windows and over onset windows only. Routing performance is measured by the number of dropped packets (total, fabric-link and host-link), packets delivered per tick, mean and 99th-percentile delivery delay, and route changes per run. The relative change in packet drops with respect to ECMP is calculated as
+Δ%=100⋅Dstrategy−DECMPDECMP,
+(26)
+The 95% confidence intervals (CIs) are obtained from 2000 paired bootstrap resamples, and calibration is measured with the expected calibration error (ECE) over ten equal-width probability bins,
+ECE=b=110nbNpb−yb,
+(27)
+where nb is the number of link-windows in bin b while p̂b and ȳb represent the average predicted probability and fraction of positive cases. Besides packet counts, two flow-level metrics are used: packet reordering (a delivered batch counts as reordered if a later-injected batch of the same flow was already delivered) and, in the closed-loop variant of Section IV-D, the flow completion time (FCT) and its slowdown relative to an idle network. 
+D. Supplementary Experiments
+The following eight supplementary experiments are conducted to address the threats to validity. Unless noted otherwise, traffic seeds 4000-4009 are used for the unseen data, and in the case of model-based methods, all three training seeds are used.
+1) Rule ablation and sweep parameters: The rule ablation process proceeds sequentially (capacity check, sequential reservation, change budget, stretch bound, minimum improvement, cooldown, and substituting the validation-tuned threshold with a fixed 0.7 value for unguarded rerouting). Afterward, all the rules are removed together to measure guarded score, guarded persistence, and guarded GNNs. In addition, parameters α, δ, and κ are swept to measure guarded score. The individual rules are also added one at a time to the controller without rules.
+2) Control loop delay: The controller and the forecaster see telemetry with staleness of dT = 5 ticks, or the route change becomes effective with a delay of dA = 5 ticks, or both delays are five ticks; the flow rates and control decisions are not delayed.
+3) Forecast interval: The forecaster is called each I = 5 ticks, and each forecast becomes actionable I ticks after being called, i.e., inference is given I ticks to complete; the controller takes decisions each tick based on the latest forecast (for model-based strategies, the first training seed is used initially).
+4) Reordering: For benchmark seeds 3000-3009, all the strategies are rerun with per-flow instrumentation that counts out-of-order deliveries (for model-based strategies, the first training seed is used); packet dynamics stay untouched.
+5) Closed-loop transport: The amount of data for each flow is fixed and equals the amount of packets offered in the open-loop scenario. A dropped packet is detected one round trip later (two times the path length in ticks), and a retransmission is started. The source rate is halved at most once each round trip after packet loss, or is increased by 5% of the nominal rate each tick in the absence of packet loss (additive increase/multiplicative decrease). The experiment ends when all flows complete (at most 600 ticks). This transport does not use ECN or delay-based congestion control and thus represents a very rough approximation of transport feedback but does not represent any protocol in particular; it is performed on benchmark seeds using the first training seed for the GNN-based strategies.
+6) Offered load: Every flow rate of the unseen traffic is multiplied by a load factor of 0.1, 0.125, 0.15, 0.2, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75 or 1.0, and ECMP, guarded score, guarded GNN (three training seeds), score without rules and the random risk signal are rerun.
+7) Control signals: The default controller is driven by a random risk drawn independently and uniformly from [0, 1] for every fabric link and tick, by a static random risk drawn once per run, by a passive oracle that reads the true score or label of every fabric link five ticks ahead with routing frozen, and by a constant risk (signal-free guard: every flow eligible, minimum improvement 0). The random and static signals and the score oracle use the score threshold 0.851, and the label oracle uses 0.5. The capacity check is also run with noisy, rate-blind and telemetry-estimated flow rates.
+8) FlowBender-style baseline: ECN marking at 10% of the buffer and rehashing after two consecutive marked ticks (also 5% and one tick), on the benchmark seeds in the open loop and on seeds 3000 - 3004 in the closed loop; the closed-loop runs of all strategies are also repeated with a reordering penalty (Section V-G).
+V. Results and Discussion
+A. Forecast Quality
+Table IV and Fig. 2 show the forecaster test results. Temporal models perform better than persistence in predicting future congestion. Persistence only repeats its current state. If we compare the AP scores for both GCN-LSTM and continuous persistence, the AP of GCN-LSTM is 0.947 +- 0.003, whereas the AP of continuous persistence is 0.880, which means GCN-LSTM has improved by +0.067 (95% CI [+0.040, +0.131]). This headline is a pooled AP, computed over all test windows of the six scenarios together (with the interval from a bootstrap over scenarios), not a mean of per-scenario APs; because AP is not additive, the pooled gain exceeds the mean of the six per-scenario gains (+0.048; Table A1). The pooled gain and its upper bound are driven mainly by the LLM-training scenario, whose AP is low for every model (0.092 for the GCN-LSTM against 0.036 for continuous persistence): without it the pooled gain is +0.052, and the bootstrap draws at or above the upper bound contain on average 3.2 copies of this scenario out of six, against one expected. Based on each test case scenario, the GCN-LSTM, on average, across three training seeds, has higher mean AP compared to the continuous persistence for all the six cases with improvements ranging from +0.023 for the elephant/mice scenario to +0.072 for the hotspot scenario (Table A1), and this also holds for the LSTM approach. The performance of the GCN-LSTM, however, is inferior to that of continuous persistence for onset event in the elephant/mice case scenario (0.107 vs. 0.137). Performance-wise, logistic regression comes very close to that of both methods; the AP for the logistic regression model is 0.938 (vs. 0.947) while its onset AP is 0.180 (vs. 0.184); logistic regression is also the better calibrated model, with a much lower ECE value of 0.011 (vs. 0.052), so most of the gain over persistence is already available from a linear model. The effect of temperature scaling is negligible as the fitted temperatures are in the interval 0.79 to 0.98 and the validation score difference due to temperature scaling is no more than 0.0016 while it is roughly 0.001 on the test set. However, the outputs are still poorly calibrated (Fig. 3): because the positive-class weight increases the prediction and temperature scaling just rescales the predictions, the mean prediction of the GCN-LSTM is higher than the positive rate (0.28 - 0.29 vs 0.233) leading to an expected calibration error (ECE) of 0.046 - 0.057 (only LSTM: 0.026 - 0.031). Applying affine (Platt) recalibration [51] to the same validation set helps eliminate the shift resulting in an ECE of 0.009 - 0.012. Therefore, we call the forecaster outputs "temperature scaled" and not probabilities; see Section V-E.

+Fig. 2. Average precision overall (a) and on congestion onsets (b). The AP of a random predictor equals the positive rate: 0.233 overall (below the axis range of (a)) and 0.035 for onsets (dashed line in (b)). Error bars show the standard deviation over three training seeds and are hidden where they are smaller than the markers. LogReg: logistic regression.
+Prediction of the occurrence of the new congestion is a difficult task. Trained model achieves only an onset AP of 0.18 - 0.19, and the edge between the GCN-LSTM and continuity is +0.021, with the confidence interval slightly more than zero. It is clear that the majority of predictive power comes from the ability to predict whether congestion will increase or decrease, but not the new congestion.
+Fig. 3. Reliability diagrams on the test scenarios for (a) the GCN-LSTM and (b) the LSTM-only forecaster, pooled over three training seeds (ten equal-width bins). Points below the diagonal indicate over-prediction. The ECE values in the legend are given as GCN-LSTM / LSTM-only.
+B. Generalisation to Unseen Workload Families
+To check whether the models learn congestion patterns instead of the traffic generator, a pilot experiment trained the models on five families and tested them on the sixth, rotating through all six. In every rotation, the learned forecaster achieved a higher AP than continuous persistence, with a confidence interval above zero, and the LSTM-only model came within 0.009 AP of the GCN-LSTM. However, the pilot ran for only 20 epochs with a single training seed, so it gives only a first, qualitative indication that the models generalise.
+Structural similarity between scenarios. Since stride generator provides the same sixteen host pairs (and consequently the same ECMP paths) in every seed, the stride test scenario contains exactly the same flows and ECMP paths as three stride training scenarios  --  there is 0.73-0.82 correlation in per-link congestion rate and 91%-94% matching link-tick labels. Similarly, LLM-training family shows the matching (label similarity 95%-97%). On the contrary, none of the other four families have more than 42% shared hosts between test and training scenarios and show no correlation in per-link congestion rate. Excluding two mentioned families from test scenarios does not alter the results: on the remaining four scenarios GCN-LSTM model beats continuous persistence by +0.052 AP (95% CI [+0.033, +0.065], an interval that is fragile with only four test scenarios), whereas its advantage in the number of onset ticks is indistinguishable from 0 (+0.013, [−0.015, +0.033]). The same is true in the holdout pilot experiment where any stride scenario is not available during training: learned forecaster again outperforms continuous persistence in predicting stride traffic (0.957 vs 0.937). This problem of structural similarity is relevant to routing experiment  --  all ten stride seeds use exactly the same set of flows due to the structure of stride benchmark.
+C. Ablation Study
+Two ablation studies were conducted to measure the contribution of the spatial component and of the forecaster.
+Without spatial information. The proposed approach employs both GCN and LSTM modules; this ablation uses only the latter to test if including the graph component helps, by comparing the full GCN-LSTM forecaster with the purely sequential LSTM forecaster. Employing only the LSTM results in performance almost equal to that of GCN-LSTM with an increase in AP by +0.001 (difference between GCN-LSTM and LSTM, 95% CI [−0.003, +0.014]). The latter approach has even a little better F1 score (0.900 vs 0.893) and Brier score (0.041 vs 0.049). The result holds for k=4 and h=5. It may be due to the fact that the short-term behavior of the link depends primarily on the flows that are using it now, and their presence is already accounted for by the link features. However, it should be noted that the forecaster has only aggregated statistics of per-link features, while the controller has access to per-flow flow counts. If flow counts or other measures of demand were available for the forecasting part, the difference could be more pronounced, and this needs to be explored further.
+Without a forecaster. To estimate the advantage of a forecaster with respect to the current state, it is necessary to run the experiment without a forecaster, i.e. to rely on model-free signals. As it can be seen from Table V, guarded score (based on the current congestion score) is the most effective in terms of drops reduction (drops 5.72%), followed by guarded LSTM (drops 5.37%), guarded GNN (drops 5.27%) and guarded persistence (drops 4.86%). Is there a real difference? Table VI examines this question: there is virtually no difference between guarded GNN and guarded LSTM with respect to the +-1% margin (90% CI [−0.28%, +0.47%] of ECMP drops on the benchmark seeds, Table VI). In addition, guarded GNN is not equivalent to guarded score within +-1% on benchmark seeds, since the 90% confidence interval goes up to 1.03% (paired) and 1.17% (family-clustered) of ECMP drops (Table VI). The meaningful result here is a one-tailed constraint: The forecaster has a maximum edge of 0.10% of ECMP drops over the current score (one-sided 95% bound), while the current score may have an edge up to approximately 1% better. For unseen seeds, the difference is even smaller  --  0.18% of ECMP drops; this value is equivalent to zero within +-1%, but only for the paired bootstrap method. It is still possible to say that guarding by the forecaster does not beat guarding by the current congestion score. From the data on family-level (Table VII), the guarded score is seen to have fewer total drops than the guarded GNN in the four families where they differ, by 0.3 to 2.0 points of the family's ECMP drops.

+D. Routing Outcomes
+Fig. 4 breaks down the drops into fabric-link drops and host-link drops. Each guarded scheme removes about 20 - 25% of the fabric-link drops, with few route changes, but every rerouting strategy also increases host-link drops, by 1.6 - 3.9% (9.0% score without the rules): traffic that is no longer dropped inside the fabric now reaches the destination host link and is dropped there, because the receiver is the bottleneck and no other path exists. Total drops therefore fall by only 4.9 - 5.7%, and the fabric-drop reduction should not be read as the headline effect. This average is primarily determined by the contribution of the Mixed family, making up 68% of ECMP drops (Table VII), and of the Hotspot case (21%), for which guarded score removes only 1.7% and 6.1%, respectively. If we consider an average with equal weights from all six cases, then guarded score filters out 28.5% of all drops, and guarded GNN 27.9%. However, this average depends significantly on the case of the single-stride collision ( - 100%), and, without that, the respective values decrease to 14.2% and 13.5%. The ECMP case sees 36% loss of offered packets, being thus highly overloaded; lighter loads are examined in Section V-F.
+Table V also shows the cost of aggressive rerouting. Unguarded GNN rerouting leads to a total drop reduction of 5.51%, outperforming three of the four guarded variants (the only one beating it is guarded score, at 5.72%) as well as reactive rerouting (4.15%), yet both aggressive techniques use over 540 route changes per run and increase delivery delay compared to ECMP (99th percentile: 17.17 ms and 17.60 ms compared to 15.47 ms). In contrast, the guarded schemes achieve drop reductions similar to these two techniques with 38 - 59 route changes per run and a lower delivery delay than ECMP and than both of them (mean: 7.26 - 7.30 ms compared to 7.70 ms; 99th percentile: 14.04 - 14.28 ms); the FlowBender-style baseline (Section V-I) has a lower 99th-percentile delay still, and against it and against the score-driven controller without rules the guarded schemes achieve only about half the drop reduction. With the same controller driven by the existing score in the absence of any admission rule (Score, no rules), 10.30% of the drops are removed, close to double the drop removal by guarded score (the difference between paired means is −4.58 points of ECMP drops, 95% CI [−7.62, −2.08]). But this method involves a total of 2,546.5 routes changed in each run and 19.5% of the packets reordered (Section V-G). It also delivers 6.2% more packets per tick than ECMP (2,662.9 against 2,508.2) and has a lower mean delay than ECMP (7.56 against 7.70 ms). The FlowBender-style baseline is better still on all four measures: on the same 60 runs it removes 0.32 points of the ECMP drops more (paired 95% CI [+0.04, +0.66]), delivers 7.6 more packets per tick ([+3.4, +12.6]) and has a 0.36 ms lower mean and a 1.57 ms lower 99th-percentile delay. In the open-loop model, FlowBender-style rehashing is therefore the best strategy on drops, throughput and 99th-percentile delay, narrowly ahead of the score-driven controller without rules on drops, with a mean delay about equal to that of the guarded schemes (Section V-I), and the guarded schemes lead only on route changes and reordering.
+
+Fig. 4. Mean drops per run split into fabric-link and host-link drops for each routing strategy; annotations give the fabric-drop change relative to ECMP.
+Fig. 5 shows the fabric-drop reduction of the guarded variants per traffic family. The highest drop reduction relative to ECMP is achieved for stride traffic, where ECMP hashes multiple flows between pods onto the same core links. All ten stride traffic seeds produce the same set of sixteen flows (only rates and timing differ); the stride result therefore represents a single collision scenario and should not be interpreted as ten independent confirmations. No drops on fabric links occur for the LLM-training family under any strategy, because its bursts overflow the links of the host nodes. For the elephant/mice family, there are fewer drops for the reactive and unguarded GNN rerouting strategies than for any guarded variant (these two strategies are not shown in Fig. 5). In the mixed family too, unguarded GNN rerouting produces fewer drops. In both families, this comes at the cost of far more route changes (roughly 7 - 10 times as many in elephant/mice and 44 - 67 times as many in mixed).
+
+Fig. 5. Fabric-drop reduction relative to ECMP per traffic family for the guarded variants. The LLM-training family has no fabric drops under any strategy.
+E. Admission-Rule Ablation and Sensitivity
+Fig. 6 evaluates the effect of disabling one admission rule at a time on traffic seeds 4000-4009 for guarded score, guarded persistence and guarded GNN. With all the rules active, the three algorithms decrease drops by 4.28%, 3.60% and 4.10%, respectively, using 34-55 route changes per run. Only the capacity check rule, when disabled, results in a meaningful change: the drop reduction shrinks by 1.2-1.4 percentage points (about a third), the number of route changes grows to 172-204 per run, and the 99th percentile delay becomes higher than in ECMP (15.7-16.4 ms vs 15.1 ms). Disabling the minimum improvement rule lowers total drops only slightly further, by 0.4-0.6 points (this range includes zero), while increasing the number of route changes 1.5-2.1 times, and disabling sequential reservation increases route changes 38-66% while leaving the drop reduction unchanged. Disabling change budget, stretch bound, cooldown, and changing the alert threshold from validation optimized score (0.851 for score and 0.75 - 0.85 for the GNN) to 0.7, changes the drop reduction by at most 0.22 points. In every case of single-rule ablation, the difference between guarded GNN and guarded score means differs by 0.05-0.63 points and not statistically significant. The leave-one-out results show only that the other rules overlap with one another, not that they are unimportant.
+
+Fig. 6. Leave-one-rule-out ablation on unseen traffic seeds 4000-4009 (60 runs per variant; guarded GNN averaged over three training seeds). (a) Change of total number of drops from the full guarding in % of mean ECMP total drops, with 95% bootstrap intervals (negative: fewer drops). (b) Number of route changes per run (log scale). (c) Mean 99th-percentile delivery delay; the dashed line corresponds to ECMP. Variant `tuned-threshold' replaces the validation threshold by 0.7 and is not applicable for binary signaling.
+The absence of rules makes visible what the price of rules is and what they accomplish. Re-routing according to the model-free signals eliminates 9.8% (score) and 9.3% (persistence) of the total drops  -  more than twice as many as the guarded controller does  -  but it involves 2,492 to 2,678 route changes per run  -  about 62 to 74 times as many  -  and lacks the delay advantage over ECMP in the 99th-percentile delay of 15.1 ms (score) and 16.5 ms (persistence), compared with 15.1 ms for ECMP. Re-routing according to the GNN forecasts that don't have the rules involved (with the validation-tuned alert threshold, unlike the unguarded GNN of Table V, which uses a fixed 0.7) is certainly worse: it removes only 4.9% of the total drops, which is 4.9 points less than score signal with the same conditions (95% CI [+3.1, +6.9]), with the highest 99th-percentile delay of all versions (17.3 ms). As the forecast triggers all the re-routes when there are no admission rules, forecast error leads directly to route change error. So admission rules don't maximize drop elimination. Rather, they set another operating point and accept some potential drop reduction for an extremely smaller number of route changes and lower delay. With it, they take little concern of the type of the signal. The gradual increase in rules in the score-based controller without rules shows how much each rule reduces the route changes (Table A2). In particular, the capacity constraint, together with the reservation in a sequential manner, change budget, minimum improvement condition, and cooldown individually bring down the number of route changes from 2,492 to between 134-583 route changes per run, while the stretch bound does not seem to affect the route changes. Out of all of those, the capacity check rule alone keeps a drop reduction of 4.64% with 134 route changes, and the minimum improvement condition alone keeps only a 1.61% drop reduction while increasing the 99th percentile delay to 16.8ms, surpassing ECMP at 15.1 ms.
+In Table VIII α, δ, κ, and M are varied one by one for guarded score on the same unseen seeds. Increasing the capacity threshold from α=0.6 to α=1.2 brings increase of both drop reduction (2.67% to 4.69%) and route changes (10 to 121 per run), but 99th-percentile delay remains below ECMP during the whole range. Complete removing of the check brings drop reduction to 3.06% with 172 route changes and increases the delay over ECMP. Decreasing δ usually causes increasing route changes for a higher drop reduction (δ=0: 4.73% with 72 changes; δ=0.5: 2.50% with 10). The cooldown has no effect until the default of five ticks, then it begins to decrease the moves and benefit of the strategy. Change budget becomes relevant only below the default: with M=2 is 0.58 points worse than with M=4, whereas M=8 and M=16 are equal (all in 0.09 points of M=4), no family differing more than 0.51 points. The current parameters lie in the middle of the trade-off of fewer drops and more route changes, not at the optimum point. None of the examined sets of the parameters nulls the benefit of ECMP, while default improves the value by -4.28% (paired 95% CI of per run difference[-12838,-6085] packet)
+Terms of the capacity check. To test whether the check works through demand or through the current queue and arrival measurements in (21), the default guard was rerun on the unseen seeds with these terms removed (Table A7): without the queue term (B), with the demand taken as the summed sending rates of the flows on the link, i.e. without the observed arrivals (C′), without both (D, a check on flow-rate demand alone), and with the rate-blind estimate of Section V-F, in which every flow is assumed to send the mean active rate, also without queue and arrivals (E; the mean is computed from the sending rates, so this check is not rate-free). With the score signal, removing the queue term changes no run, and removing the arrivals as well changes total drops by +0.15 points of the ECMP drops (95% CI [−0.20, +0.53], family-clustered [−0.25, +0.64]; −4.13% against −4.28% for the full check), with 35 instead of 34 route changes per run. With the random signal, the check on flow-rate demand alone gives −4.33% against −4.59% (+0.26 points, [−0.36, +0.93]) with 132 instead of 120 route changes, and the rate-blind check gives −4.66% (−4.40% with the score). None of these intervals excludes zero, so the removed terms have no detectable effect on drops; they are not shown to be equivalent. Re-evaluating every capacity test of the full check under the reduced checks, on the same state and along the full check's own run, shows why the effect is small: with the score signal, no candidate path that reaches the check contains a queued link, because a link with a queue is near full utilisation and therefore has a high score, which the link costs and the minimum-improvement rule already avoid, and the observed arrivals decide 1.4% of the tests; with the random signal, 46% of the tested candidates contain a queued link, but on such links the summed rates or arrivals already exceed the headroom, so the queue term alone decides fewer than 0.1% of the tests and the arrivals 2.2%. The check on flow-rate demand alone still removes 1.07 (score; 95% CI [−0.13, +2.29]) and 2.75 points (random; [+1.43, +4.26]) more drops than no capacity check (−3.06% and −1.57%), with 35 against 172 and 132 against 294 route changes per run. In this simulator, the effect of the capacity check is therefore carried by the demand that the flows' sending rates place on each link, and, since the rate-blind check also works, mainly by where the flows are rather than by their exact rates; the queue and arrival measurements add no detectable benefit. Current congestion still enters the score-driven guard through its signal, which steers candidate paths away from loaded links, but the random signal, which carries no congestion information, reaches a drop reduction that is not detectably different with the same demand-only check. A deployed controller would have to estimate this demand from measurements, so the separation may be less clean in practice.
