+Calibration and routing. Replacing the temperature-scaled GNN probabilities by Platt-recalibrated ones, with the alert threshold re-tuned on validation data, changes the total-drop reduction of guarded GNN on the unseen seeds from 4.10% to 4.06% (paired difference +0.04 points, 95% CI [−0.34, +0.44]), with 57 instead of 55 route changes per run. The routing conclusions therefore do not depend on the miscalibration reported in Section V-A.
+
+Fig. 7. Change in total drops relative to ECMP versus route changes per run (log scale). The curve shows the sensitivity analysis of guarded score over M∈{2,4,8,16} on unseen traffic seeds. In the legend, max_changes denotes M.
+F. Delay, Control Signals and Offered Load
+The simulator provides the controller with perfect and instantaneous information: telemetry arrives at the same tick as described, and the route change applies at the subsequent tick. In real-world system, delays will occur due to export, computation, and rule installation. This is precisely the role of the forecaster to be played. Fig. 8 reproduces the guarded score and guarded GNN comparison on the test seeds and with telemetry delays of two and five ticks and actuation delays of two and five ticks; in the case of d_T = 5, five-tick forecast means the current tick, while the score is five ticks behind.
+Up to five ticks of delay does not significantly alter the drop reduction provided by both approaches (less than one percentage point and not monotonically, Fig. 8a). With telemetry delay of two ticks the score achieves 4.82% drops and with five-tick telemetry delay, it is 4.45%; similarly, for actuation delays of two and five ticks the score delivers 4.16% and 4.18% drops. The guarded GNN approach provides 4.49%, 4.65%, 3.97% and 3.46% drops respectively. Difference between GNN forecasting and score is indicative of marginal gain only under a five-tick delay between telemetry and forecast -- that is, when GNN forecasts coincide with the current time step (GNN minus score: -0.20 points, 95% CI [-0.55; +0.12]) where the confidence interval encompasses zero. Under five-tick delay between actuation and forecast, when forecast is thought to be the most beneficial for performance, the GNN lags behind the score by 0.72 points ([-0.01; +1.51]), and when both actuation and telemetry delays are taken into account, GNN underperforms by 0.20 points ([-0.25; +0.67]). No indication of benefit from forecasting is provided by the sweep under both telemetry and actuation delay settings, and actuation delay setting runs counter to forecast expectations; the reason has not been explored.
+Control signals. The existence of stale telemetry does not affect the observed reduction of the guarded score (4.82% and 4.45% for telemetry delays of two and five ticks, respectively, versus 4.28% without any delay). For that reason, the default controller was also tested on unseen seeds with two control signals and an oracle. If the risk for each link is randomly selected independently for each tick, then the overall number of drops is reduced by 4.59% using 120 route changes per run (difference between guarded score: -0.31 points, 95% confidence interval [-1.18, +0.53]). Reductions per family are similar to those of the guarded score. The two signals are not equivalent on churn, however: the random signal needs 3.5 times as many route changes as the score (120 against 34 per run; paired difference +86, 95% CI [+69, +105]), reorders 0.67% of the delivered packets in 23.6% of the flows against 0.22% in 7.4%, and has a 0.43 ms higher 99th-percentile delay (95% CI [+0.02, +0.83]). The GNN needs 1.6 times as many route changes as the score (55 per run; +21, [+11, +34]) and reorders 0.27% of the packets in 8.8% of the flows (Table A3). At equal drops, the signal therefore still controls churn, and on this measure the forecaster is worse than the current score. The static random risk per run moves almost no flows (6 route changes per run) and shows no effect. A passive oracle, computed at every tick by copying the simulator state, advancing the copy five ticks with all routes frozen (no further controller moves) and reading the true future score or label of every fabric link, reduces the number of drops by 3.57% and 3.68%, i.e. 0.71 and 0.60 points less than the current score (95% CIs [−0.24, +1.72] and [−0.42, +1.79]), with 22 and 29 route changes per run, fewer than the score, and 0.18% and 0.21% of the packets reordered. Because it knows only what will be congested if no flow is moved, and not what happens after a move, this oracle is not an upper bound on the value of forecasting; it shows only that a perfect passive five-tick congestion label adds nothing to the guarded controller. The drop reduction, based on the standard rule set, is therefore independent of any information about congestion in the risk signal; it comes from changing traffic flows from their current ECMP allocation, as a function of the capacity test performed at each link on the demand placed on it by the flows (Section V-E). Under the admission rules, the controller therefore acts as a demand-aware reshuffler in the manner of Hedera rather than as a congestion-driven controller, and comparisons of GNN and score under the guard cannot reveal the value of forecasting, because every signal reaches the same floor of drop reduction. The signal matters only without the rules, and there the forecaster removes far fewer drops than the score (4.9% against 9.8%).
+Rate knowledge and a signal-free guard. The capacity check (21) uses the flow rates and the demand on each link, which the simulator knows exactly (the demand is the larger of the summed flow rates and the observed arrivals). The variants below change only the flow-rate estimates and keep the queue and arrival terms; Section V-E removes those terms. To test whether the benefit depends on this idealisation, the default controller was rerun on the unseen seeds with rate estimates carrying multiplicative log-normal noise (σ = 0.25, 0.5 and 1.0, mean-preserving, drawn afresh every tick), with a rate-blind check that assumes every active flow sends the mean active rate, and with telemetry-estimated rates, i.e. the mean number of packets each flow delivered per tick over the last five ticks (Table A4). The drop reduction does not shrink: guarded score reduces total drops by 5.06%, 4.81% and 4.36% with noisy rates, by 4.38% when rate-blind and by 5.72% with telemetry estimates, against 4.28% with exact rates, but its route changes rise from 34 to 44 - 103 per run. Delivered rates are lower than sending rates whenever packets are dropped, so telemetry estimates loosen the check; with them, the random risk signal removes 7.57% of the total drops, 1.85 points more than the score (95% CI of the difference [+0.46, +3.49]). A signal-free guard, in which every flow is eligible, candidates are plain shortest paths and the capacity check alone decides, reduces total drops by 3.49% with exact rates (0.79 points less than guarded score, 95% CI [−0.24, +1.87]) and by 6.80% with telemetry estimates, with 178 and 209 route changes per run. In this model, the capacity check therefore does not need exact per-flow rates and provides most of the drop reduction without any risk signal, and the signal mainly affects how many route changes are made. Real demand estimators may err in ways that independent noise does not capture.
+Offered load.  To cover light to heavy load, every flow rate of the unseen traffic was multiplied by a load factor from 0.1 to 1.0, where 1.0 is the traffic used elsewhere (Table A5). This lowers the share of offered packets that ECMP drops from 35.5% at the default load to 26.2%, 15.4%, 4.6%, 1.8%, 0.32%, 0.14% and 0% at load factors 0.75, 0.5, 0.25, 0.2, 0.15, 0.125 and 0.1. At load factors of 0.5 and above, guarded score removes 4.3 - 5.6% of the total drops. At 0.25 and below it removes none: it has 2.97% more drops than ECMP at 0.25 and 1.56% more at 0.2, and is within 0.4% of ECMP at 0.15 and 0.125, with 0 - 24 route changes per run and a 99th-percentile delay at or below that of ECMP. The forecaster does not outperform the score at any load: guarded GNN minus guarded score lies between 0.00 and +0.62 points of the ECMP drops, every 95% interval includes zero, and near the congestion knee (load factors 0.15, 0.2 and 0.25) the differences are +0.62, +0.05 and +0.31 points. The random risk signal stays within 1.7 points of the score at every load, again with intervals that include zero, but it moves about 290 flows per run even at light load, which raises its 99th-percentile delay 0.9 - 1.7 ms above that of ECMP at load factors up to 0.25. Separately, the score-driven controller without admission rules removes 2.2 - 11.3 points more drops than guarded score at every load with drops, with 12 - 2,492 route changes per run. Within this range of loads, the sweep gives no indication of a load region in which forecasting helps; the GNN was trained at the default load only.
+
+Fig. 8. Control-loop delay on the unseen traffic seeds 4000 - 4009: (a) change in total drops relative to ECMP and (b) paired difference between guarded GNN and guarded score, in percentage points of the mean ECMP total drops, with 95% paired-bootstrap intervals; dT: telemetry delay, dA: actuation delay (ticks); guarded GNN averaged over three training seeds.
+G. Flow-Level Effects
+Reordering. Route change may reorder packets if packets on the new route pass by packets yet waiting on the old one. For the test seed sequences, the aggressive policies reorder 15.7-18.2% of the packets, while 53-64% of the flows incur reordering (score, no rules: 19.5% and 68%); the guarded policies reorder 0.26-0.38% of the packets in 9-14% of the flows, while ECMP does not reorder any packets at all. Thus, the guard decreases reordering by a factor of 41-71. It is significant because reordering is the primary expense incurred by mid-path rerouting for loss-sensitive transports, which treat reordering as loss.
+Closed-loop flow transport and flow completion time. Table IX shows the closed-loop version of the experiment described in Sec. IV-D, where lost packets are retried, and senders back off. All guarded approaches yield shorter flow completion times than ECMP, as the mean FCT drops to 120.3-120.6 ticks, while the median slow-down decreases from 1.37 to 1.26-1.29, the mean run-wise slowdown by 0.06-0.07 (95% CI for paired difference in guarded GNN slow-down: [-0.136, -0.009]). Aggressive rerouting leads to the opposite effect  -  both reactive and unguarded GNN rerouting increase the mean FCT up to 137.5 ticks and 131.9 ticks correspondingly with on average around 1500 route changes per run, since this strategy incurs additional packet losses and retries (in 49% and 42% of the packets are retransmitted, whereas only 39% are for ECMP). The rule-less, score-based controller, which removes the second-most drops in the open-loop test (after the FlowBender-style baseline), executes 6,304 route changes per run and has a mean FCT 1.3% longer than ECMP (counting unfinished flows as censored; paired-cell bootstrap 95% CI [−1.1%, +4.1%]) but a p99 FCT 7.0% shorter ([−9.3%, +1.2%]); neither difference is significant, so against this strategy the FCT evidence is mixed. The same holds for the FlowBender-style baseline (Section V-I): the guard shortens the mean FCT but not the 99th-percentile FCT relative to it. The benefit of the guard is especially pronounced in the case of elephant/mice family (mean slow-down 1.66 for ECMP compared to 1.33-1.39); in the incast-dominated hotspot family, where the receiver's link is the bottleneck, guarded rerouting slightly increases the mean slow-down (from 2.71 to 2.75-2.79), the LLM-training family is not impacted. Guarded GNN and guarded score are indistinguishable at the mean (average FCT of 120.3 and 120.4 ticks), but their p99 FCTs are different (444 and 456 ticks). Hence, the open-loop experimental results are confirmed by the coarse closed-loop model as well; indeed, admission control transforms reactive and unguarded GNN rerouting from being detrimental to beneficial for the flow completion, while forecaster has no effect here; against the score-driven controller without rules, the base model cannot separate the two. The base model includes no penalty for reordering, so its FCTs omit the main cost of rerouting; the variant below adds one. Between 6 and 9 out of 1,928 flows per approach had not completed in 600 ticks and hence excluded from the FCT computation. Counting the censored connections in the end (at 600 ticks) contributes to increasing the average FCT of all strategies by 1.4 - 2.0 ticks and the p99 FCT by 13 - 32 ticks, without changing the overall ranking (ECMP: 126.6 and 502 ticks; guarded strategies: 121.7-122.0 and 457-475 ticks).
+Reordering penalty. The closed-loop runs were repeated with a conservative reordering penalty: every out-of-order delivery is treated by the sender as a loss signalled by duplicate acknowledgements, so half a round trip later the reordered packets are retransmitted spuriously (they consume capacity but add no progress) and the rate is halved, at most once per round trip together with real losses (Table A6). The guarded strategies are barely affected: their spurious retransmissions are 0.3% of the offered packets, and the mean FCT of guarded score stays 3.5% below that of ECMP (95% CI [−5.1%, −2.1%]; p99 4.4% below, [−8.0%, −0.4%]). Without admission rules, spurious retransmissions reach about 16% of the offered packets, and the mean FCT of the score-driven controller rises to 18.8% above ECMP ([+15.6%, +21.8%]; p99 +6.1%, [+1.7%, +14.2%]), while reactive and unguarded GNN rerouting reach 26.6% and 22.9% above ECMP. Because this penalty treats every reordered batch as a loss, whereas real transports tolerate some reordering (duplicate-ACK thresholds, time-based loss detection), the two models bracket the realistic case: the guard is clearly preferable when reordering costs as much as loss, and indistinguishable from the score-driven controller without rules when reordering is free. Which applies depends on the transport and cannot be settled in this model.

+H. Computation Time and Control Interval
+Figure 9 shows inference time per tick for the laptop. Inference time of the GCN-LSTM is 2.0 ms (median) and 4.1 ms (99th percentile), while the inference time of the LSTM-only model is 1.5 ms (99th percentile). In the routing experiments, the forecaster is called at every 1-millisecond tick, but the simulator does not wait for the forecaster to finish. Hence, the GCN-LSTM model is slower than the simulated clock on this CPU because the median inference time exceeds the duration of one tick. It is the tick and not the horizon that is the right basis of comparison here.
+
+Fig. 9. Median (p50) and 99th-percentile (p99) inference time per tick for the GCN-LSTM and LSTM-only forecasters relative to the 5 ms forecast horizon (laptop CPU, two threads).
+Inference is also not the limiting factor here. As measured on the cloud machine using the original controller, one tick of the guarded controller requires 3.4 ms in median and 69 ms in 99% percentile when driven by the score, and 2.7 ms in median and 82 ms in 99% percentile when driven by the GCN-LSTM, while the GCN-LSTM takes 2.9 ms on average in median on the same cloud machine; in the case of the mixed family, one tick takes 44 - 51 ms in median. A breakdown of the costs of running the guarded controller with GCN-LSTM on the mixed network revealed that almost all the costs are due to computing weighted shortest paths in NetworkX. The controller, therefore, is the bottleneck of the control loop and a deployment solution requires either the computation of paths off-line or some hardware support.
+To see whether coarser-grained forecasting maintains the routing result, guarded GNN was tested again, but in this run, the forecaster is called only every two or five ticks and its prediction is usable only after its interval, thus, allowing 2 or 5 ms per invocation for the GCN-LSTM to perform inference. This changes the reduction in total drops of the controlled runs on unseen seeds from 4.14% (in the case of the original guarded controller, calling the GCN-LSTM every tick and with the same training seed) to 4.33% and 4.48%, with the number of route changes going down to 53 and 36 compared with 68. Thus, when predicting the future every five ticks, which even allows time enough for the 99% percentile GCN-LSTM inference on the laptop computer (taking 4.1 ms), the routing result stays preserved, and in this coarseness the GCN-LSTM inference stays in line with the simulated clock. Still, the complete duration of the controller tick (2.7 - 3.4 ms at the median, 44 - 51 ms within the mixed family) is out of reach.
+I. Comparison with the State of the Art
+Load-balancing solutions relying on switches (CONGA, HULA, and Clove) and those implemented on the hosts (FlowBender, Hermes) are not reproduced in the simulation because flowlets, ECN marking, and transport protocol behaviors are lacking; a FlowBender-style emulation with ECN marking at a queue threshold and rehashing after consecutive congested ticks is evaluated below. Thus, the quantitative part of this comparison will be based on baseline schemes capturing major design ideas: static hashing (ECMP), reactive threshold rerouting, unguarded forecast-driven rerouting, and model-free guarded rerouting. The model-free guarded controller follows the reservation rule set by Hedera and also demonstrates the dampened and conditional movements associated with FlowBender and Hermes; however, it is not a replacement of those systems in terms of its functioning process.
+FlowBender-style baseline. Since the required state exists in the simulator, a FlowBender-style baseline [20] was implemented: a link marks ECN when its queue exceeds 10% of its buffer, and a flow whose path contains a marking link for two consecutive ticks is rehashed to a randomly chosen different equal-cost path; there is no capacity check, budget or risk signal. On the benchmark seeds (Table V) it reduces total drops by 10.62%, 4.90 points more than guarded score (95% CI of the difference [+2.25, +8.09]), delivers the most packets per tick of all strategies (2,670.5; 76 more than guarded score, paired 95% CI [+36, +124]) and has the lowest delivery delay (mean 7.21 ms, 99th percentile 13.58 ms; 0.53 ms below guarded score at the 99th percentile, [−0.90, −0.15], whereas the mean differs by only −0.05 ms, [−0.13, +0.01]), but it makes 1,039 route changes per run and reorders 9.4% of the delivered packets in 42% of the flows; a more aggressive setting (5% threshold, one tick) gives 10.79% with 2,159 route changes. In the closed-loop model its mean FCT is 3.4% longer than that of guarded score without a reordering penalty ([+1.8%, +5.3%]) and 12.4% longer with it ([+9.8%, +14.5%]; Table A6), but without the penalty its 99th-percentile FCT is 2.5% shorter (459 against 471 ticks, [−8.0%, +1.4%]); only the mean favours the guard. FlowBender-style rehashing therefore behaves like the score-driven controller without rules, and a simple queue-threshold rehash beats the guard on open-loop drops, throughput and 99th-percentile delay: the guard is not the open-loop performance winner. Its case rests on 18 - 27 times fewer route changes and 25 - 36 times less reordering (0.26 - 0.38% of the packets), which in the closed-loop model give a shorter mean but not a shorter tail flow completion time.
+NeuralFabric is distinct from reactive approaches of Table I in its ability to rely on forecasts and is different from learning solutions like RouteNet and T-GCN that are assessed through their ability to predict or optimize routes rather than perform online rerouting. Our results show that, under the conditions of a millisecond time scale, the guarded controller does not show any loss in performance using the current score instead of predictions; whether the same holds for CONGA, FlowBender, and Hermes was not tested.
+J. Discussion
+Key results. Temporal neural networks were shown to predict the future state of link congestions better than persistence in overall AP in all the settings used, including a pilot experiment evaluating workload families not seen in the training set and without any structural repeating cases of stride and LLM-training. There is no gain from use of Graph Convolutional Network (GCN). Prediction of new congestion appears to be hard and predictions suffer from the upward bias of temperature-scaled probabilities. Forecast-driven rerouting does not beat current congestion-dependent rerouting with identical admission rules in all single-rule ablation experiments and in presence of delays of up to five telemetry or actuation steps. All guarded schemes deliver a 2.9 - 3.4% gain in the number of packets delivered per tick, reduce delay and drop totals by 4.9 - 5.7% while making less reordering actions than aggressive rerouting, despite the fact that most of total drops are incurred on the host links and are beyond control by any rerouting mechanism. A simple FlowBender-style queue-threshold rehash, however, beats every guarded scheme on open-loop drops, delivered packets and 99th-percentile delay (Section V-I), so the case for the guard rests on its far smaller number of route changes and amount of reordering and on the mean flow completion time in the closed-loop model, not on superior open-loop drops, throughput or delay. They are also shown to decrease flow completion time in our closed loop model setting, unlike reactive and unguarded GNN rerouting, which increases completion times; against the score-driven controller without rules, the guard shortens completion times only when reordering is penalised.
+Interpretation. The guarded rerouting procedure is not the best policy for drop avoidance: removing all admission rules from the model-free controller doubles its drop reduction, and a FlowBender-style rehash does slightly better still, but this policy changes 62 - 74 times as many routes and loses most of the guard's delay advantage over ECMP. Thus, the admission rules define the tradeoff point between drops, churn, and delay; the removal of capacity check alone becomes the major alteration in this respect; nevertheless, gradual integration of the rules reveals their redundancy with each other (Section V-E). Considering that the five-tick horizon is small relative to the lifetime of the flows and primary control loop lacks of delay, the contribution of the risk signal becomes marginal in case of adoption of the capacity check, since stochastic risk signal works as well as the existing one on drops, though with 3.5 times as many route changes, and a perfect passive five-tick congestion label brings no improvement over it (V-F). The congestion measurements inside the check itself also add no detectable benefit: a check on the demand placed on each link by the flows' sending rates and paths, without queue and arrival terms, gives a drop reduction that is not detectably different (Section V-E). What matters in this setting is information about where the flows are and what they send, rather than measured queues or arrivals, used as a gate on where they may move; congestion information used as a trigger for which flows move does not change drops. On the other hand, without admission rules, risk signal gains importance and increases in forecast error is the factor increasing the number of moves. Under such circumstances, forecast-based routing becomes 4.9 percentage points worse than score-based routing.
+Implications. With respect to a millisecond-scale control loop equipped with an admission control policy, the priority should lie in improving the admission control policy rather than on improving the forecaster itself. Forecast-driven routing should be compared with a model-free signal that drives the same action, and each admission rule should be ablated. Improvements in prediction may otherwise be incorrectly identified as an improvement in admission control policies. These considerations depend on the particular experimental setting, and thus generalization of these findings is not possible to control loops where delays are longer with respect to the forecasted horizon, to wider fabrics, or to traffic with a longer lifespan. The delay sweep does not indicate any benefit of forecasting at delays of up to five ticks; with five ticks of actuation delay, the forecaster did worse by 0.72 points. 
+Limitations. Packets-batches simulator operates with 1 ms tick rate and does not consider ECN, PFC, and RDMA. The closed-loop configuration used in simulations for flow completion times is a coarser, loss-based model as opposed to the real transport protocol. The telemetry in question is the ideal case in the main set of experiments; the span of the delay sweep consists of constant delays up to five ticks. Traffic is synthetic; the traffic stride and LLM training families have repeated host pairs' usage within each seed while the former is based on the pattern of collective communication rather than on traces replication. Only one topology (k = 4) and one forecast horizon (five ticks) were investigated; the forecasting data set consists of six cases, holdout experiments were performed using one seed only, and the forecaster employs only per-link aggregated features. Some additional experiments use a single seed of GNN training. The capacity check is given exact flow rates in the main experiments; noisy, rate-blind and telemetry-estimated rates were tested (Section V-F), but not a deployed demand estimator; the finding that the queue and arrival terms add nothing (Section V-E) rests on exact knowledge of each flow's path and sending rate.
+Recommendations. Larger fabric spatial graph models (for instance, k = 8) should be validated on longer forecasting horizons with per-flow demand and interlink congestion propagation where neighboring information has more relevance. In order to produce forecasts valuable beyond state information, onset-oriented training objectives and adjusted probabilities would become necessary. Forecast-based control could be reconsidered with real transport protocols, where control loop delays would be estimated on hardware with forecasting horizons matching the delays.
+VI. Conclusion and Future Scope
+NeuralFabric examines forecast-driven rerouting in a controlled setting in which a telemetry-driven congestion forecaster and model-free signals drive the same capacity-guarded controller in a full-duplex Fat-Tree simulation. The learned forecasters ranked future congestion better than persistence in overall AP in every test scenario (on average over training seeds), though not in onset AP, but a per-link logistic regression obtained most of this gain, the spatial graph component gave no measurable advantage, onsets remained hard to predict and the probabilities were biased upwards. Forecast-driven guarded routing performed no better than the same controller driven by the current congestion score, on the benchmark and on unseen traffic, under every single-rule ablation and with up to five ticks of telemetry or actuation delay or at lighter offered loads. All guarded approaches increased delivered packets per tick by 2.9 - 3.4% and reduced pooled total drops by 4.9 - 5.7% (fabric-link drops by 20 - 25%), with about ten times fewer route changes and 41 - 71 times less packet reordering than aggressive rerouting, with lower delay than ECMP and, in a closed-loop transport model, with about 4% shorter mean flow completion times; against the score-driven controller without rules, this advantage appears only when reordering is penalised. A FlowBender-style queue-threshold rehash removed the most drops (10.6%) and beat the guard on open-loop drops, throughput and 99th-percentile delay, with 18 - 27 times as many route changes, 25 - 36 times as much reordering and a longer mean flow completion time; the guard is therefore not the best open-loop strategy, and its case rests on churn, reordering and mean flow completion. The ablations show that removing the capacity check alone changes the outcome most, that the rules are largely redundant with one another, and that the rules as a whole trade part of the attainable drop reduction for far fewer route changes and lower delay. The main point is that, in this idealised millisecond-scale setting, with the admission rules in place, the guarded controller acts as a demand-aware reshuffler whose benefit comes from the capacity check that gates where flows may move rather than from congestion information in the signal that selects which flows move; removing the queue and arrival measurements from the check changed total drops by at most 0.26 points of the ECMP drops on average, with no detectable effect, so the check works through the demand that the flows place on each link: a random risk signal did as well as the current score on drops, though with 3.5 times as many route changes, and a perfect passive five-tick congestion label added nothing, whereas without the rules the signal matters; whether forecasting pays off in slower control loops, at longer horizons or in larger fabrics remains open.
+Future work will test larger topologies (k=8) and longer forecast horizons matched to measured control-loop delays, use realistic flow sizes and transport protocols with lossless flow control, give the forecaster per-flow demand inputs, design training objectives for congestion onsets, and implement the controller with precomputed candidate paths on programmable data planes.
+Data and Code Availability
+The simulator, training and evaluation code, checkpoints of the trained models, the raw results of every run (including the additional experiments), and the scripts that generate the figures and tables are available from the authors upon reasonable request. All datasets and traffic are generated with fixed random seeds and can be regenerated exactly with the generation scripts; the simulator is deterministic.

+"""Concern 1 (fourth review): which terms of the capacity check matter?  Unseen seeds 4000-4009, six families,
+score and random risk signals, everything else at the defaults.  Results -> results/rev5_cap.jsonl (resumable).
+
+Capacity check of the guarded controller (paper Eq. 21), per directed link e on the candidate path:
+    D(e) + r_f + Q(e)/dt <= alpha * C(e),  with  D(e) = max(R(e), A(e))
+  R(e) = sum of the sending rates of the active flows whose current path uses e   [packets/tick]
+  A(e) = packets that arrived at e in the last tick (observed arrivals)          [packets/tick]
+  Q(e) = queue of e in packets, dt = 1 tick;  C(e) in packets/tick;  alpha = 0.90
+Variants (only these terms change):
+  A   D = max(R, A), queue term kept         (the paper's check)
+  B   D = max(R, A), queue term removed
+  Cp  D = R,         queue term kept         (observed arrivals removed)
+  D   D = R,         queue term removed      (demand-only check)
+  E   as D, but every flow's rate is replaced by the mean active rate (existing rate-blind estimator);
+      supplementary: the mean is still computed from the flows' sending rates
+  nocap  no capacity check at all (alpha = None), the leave-one-rule-out reference
+plus ECMP.  The random signal and its seeds (1000 * family index + seed) are those of rev4_c1."""
+from paths import NF_ROOT  # folder locations (see paths.py)
+import json, os, sys, time, traceback
+import multiprocessing as mp
+
+HERE = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))
+OUT = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(HERE), 'results', 'rev5_cap.jsonl')
+FAM = ['uniform', 'hotspot', 'elephant_mice', 'stride', 'llm_training', 'mixed']
+SEEDS = list(range(4000, 4010))
+# switch names follow the local nf_exp.py (cap_arrivals / cap_queue, defaults True = paper's check)
+VARIANTS = {'A': {}, 'B': dict(cap_queue=False), 'Cp': dict(cap_arrivals=False),
+            'D': dict(cap_arrivals=False, cap_queue=False),
+            'E': dict(cap_arrivals=False, cap_queue=False, rate_mode='blind'),
+            'nocap': dict(headroom=None)}
+KEEP = ['total_drops', 'fabric_link_drops', 'host_link_drops', 'total_offered', 'total_delivered',
+        'delivered_mean_latency_us', 'delivered_p99_latency_us', 'reroutes', 'reordered_packets',
+        'reordered_fraction', 'flows_with_reordering', 'n_flows', 'packet_conservation', 'drop_split_ok']
+
+
+def tasks():
+    T = []
+    for fi, fam in enumerate(FAM):
+        for s in SEEDS:
+            base = dict(family=fam, seed=s, noise_seed=1000 * fi + s)
+            T.append(dict(base, variant='ecmp', signal=None))
+            for v, g in VARIANTS.items():
+                for sig in ('score', 'random'):
+                    T.append(dict(base, variant=v, signal=sig, threshold=0.851, guard=dict(g)))
+    for t in T:
+        t['id'] = '|'.join(str(t.get(k)) for k in ('variant', 'signal', 'family', 'seed'))
+    return T
+
+
+_W = {}
+
+
+def _init():
+    sys.path.insert(0, HERE)
+    import nf_exp
+    from simulation.topology import build_fat_tree
+    _W['X'], _W['g'] = nf_exp, build_fat_tree()
+
+
+def _run(t):
+    try:
+        X, g = _W['X'], _W['g']
+        from simulation.traffic_generator import TrafficGenerator
+        flows = TrafficGenerator(g, seed=t['seed']).generate_scenario(total_ticks=150, pattern=t['family'])
+        if t['variant'] == 'ecmp':
+            r = X.run_case_x(g, flows, 150, 'ecmp', instrument=True)
+        else:
+            guard = dict(t['guard'], noise_seed=t['noise_seed'], signal=t['signal'])
+            r = X.run_case_x(g, flows, 150, 'guarded', threshold=t['threshold'], guard=guard,
+                             rng_seed=t['noise_seed'], instrument=True)
+        out = {k: t.get(k) for k in ('id', 'variant', 'signal', 'family', 'seed')}
+        out.update({k: r[k] for k in KEEP})
+        out['delivered_per_tick'] = r['total_delivered'] / 150
+        return out
+    except Exception:
+        return dict(id=t['id'], error=traceback.format_exc())
+
+
+if __name__ == '__main__':
+    os.environ.setdefault('NF_ROOT', NF_ROOT)
+    done = {json.loads(l)['id'] for l in open(OUT)} if os.path.exists(OUT) else set()
+    T = [t for t in tasks() if t['id'] not in done]
+    if len(sys.argv) > 2 and sys.argv[2] == 'smoke':
+        T = [t for t in T if t['family'] == 'hotspot' and t['seed'] == 4000]
+    print(len(T), 'tasks', flush=True)
+    t0 = time.time()
+    with mp.Pool(int(sys.argv[1]) if len(sys.argv) > 1 else 2, initializer=_init) as pool, open(OUT, 'a') as fh:
+        for i, r in enumerate(pool.imap_unordered(_run, T), 1):
+            fh.write(json.dumps(r) + '\n'); fh.flush()
+            if i % 100 == 0 or i == len(T):
+                el = (time.time() - t0) / 60
+                print(f'  {i}/{len(T)} {el:.1f} min, eta {el / i * (len(T) - i):.1f} min', flush=True)

+++ b/revision/scripts/rev5_cap_analyze.py

+"""Summary of the capacity-check ablation (rev5_cap.jsonl) -> results/rev5_cap_summary.json
+Differences are paired over the 60 (family, seed) cells, in points of the mean ECMP total drops (the paper's
+convention), with the paired bootstrap (2,000 draws, seed 42) and the family-clustered bootstrap."""
+import json, os
+import numpy as np
+
+R = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))), 'results')
+rows = [json.loads(l) for l in open(os.path.join(R, 'rev5_cap.jsonl'))]
+assert not [r for r in rows if 'error' in r], 'failed runs'
+assert all(r['packet_conservation'] and r['drop_split_ok'] for r in rows), 'invariant violated'
+cell = {}
+for r in rows:
+    cell.setdefault((r['variant'], r['signal']), {})[(r['family'], r['seed'])] = r
+assert all(len(v) == 60 for v in cell.values()), {k: len(v) for k, v in cell.items()}
+ecmp = cell[('ecmp', None)]
+E = np.mean([x['total_drops'] for x in ecmp.values()])
+
+
+def boot(d, draws=2000, seed=42, cluster=False):
+    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
+    if not cluster:
+        v = np.array([d[k] for k in sorted(d)])
+        out = v[rng.integers(0, len(v), size=(draws, len(v)))].mean(1)
+    else:
+        fams = sorted({f for f, _ in d})
+        byf = {f: np.array([d[k] for k in sorted(d) if k[0] == f]) for f in fams}
+        out = np.empty(draws)
+        for i in range(draws):
+            pick = rng.integers(0, len(fams), size=len(fams))
+            out[i] = np.mean([byf[fams[j]][rng.integers(0, len(byf[fams[j]]), size=len(byf[fams[j]]))].mean() for j in pick])
+    return np.percentile(out, [2.5, 97.5]).round(2).tolist()
+
+
+def diff(a, b, key='total_drops', scale=None):
+    """paired a - b; drops in points of mean ECMP drops, other metrics in their own units"""
+    sc = (100 / E) if scale is None else scale
+    d = {c: (a[c][key] - b[c][key]) * sc for c in a}
+    return dict(mean=round(float(np.mean(list(d.values()))), 2), ci95=boot(d), ci95_cluster=boot(d, cluster=True))
+
+
+S = dict(ecmp_mean_drops=float(E), n_cells=60, cells={}, effects={})
+for (v, sig), a in sorted(cell.items(), key=lambda kv: str(kv[0])):
+    m = lambda k: float(np.mean([x[k] for x in a.values()]))
+    o = dict(total_pct=(m('total_drops') / E - 1) * 100,
+             fabric_pct=(m('fabric_link_drops') / np.mean([x['fabric_link_drops'] for x in ecmp.values()]) - 1) * 100,
+             host_pct=(m('host_link_drops') / np.mean([x['host_link_drops'] for x in ecmp.values()]) - 1) * 100,
+             delivered_per_tick=m('delivered_per_tick'), mean_ms=m('delivered_mean_latency_us') / 1000,
+             p99_ms=m('delivered_p99_latency_us') / 1000, reroutes=m('reroutes'),
+             reordered_pct=float(np.sum([x['reordered_packets'] for x in a.values()]) /
+                                 np.sum([x['total_delivered'] for x in a.values()]) * 100))
+    if v != 'ecmp':
+        o['vs_ecmp'] = diff(a, ecmp)
+    S['cells'][f'{v}|{sig}'] = {k: (float(x) if isinstance(x, float) else x) for k, x in o.items()}   # unrounded: round once, when formatting
+
+C = lambda v, s: cell[(v, s)]
+for sig in ('score', 'random'):
+    S['effects'][sig] = {
+        'B-A (remove queue term)': diff(C('B', sig), C('A', sig)),
+        'Cp-A (remove arrivals)': diff(C('Cp', sig), C('A', sig)),
+        'D-A (remove both)': diff(C('D', sig), C('A', sig)),
+        'D-B (remove arrivals, no queue)': diff(C('D', sig), C('B', sig)),
+        'D-Cp (remove queue, no arrivals)': diff(C('D', sig), C('Cp', sig)),
+        'E-D (rate-blind vs exact rates)': diff(C('E', sig), C('D', sig)),
+        'E-A (rate-blind demand only)': diff(C('E', sig), C('A', sig)),
+        'nocap-A (remove the check)': diff(C('nocap', sig), C('A', sig)),
+        'nocap-D (remove demand-only check)': diff(C('nocap', sig), C('D', sig)),
+        'reroutes D-A': diff(C('D', sig), C('A', sig), 'reroutes', 1),
+        'p99_ms D-A': diff(C('D', sig), C('A', sig), 'delivered_p99_latency_us', 1e-3),
+    }
+S['effects']['random-score'] = {v: diff(C(v, 'random'), C(v, 'score')) for v in ('A', 'B', 'Cp', 'D', 'E', 'nocap')}
+S['effects']['random-score reroutes'] = {v: diff(C(v, 'random'), C(v, 'score'), 'reroutes', 1) for v in ('A', 'B', 'Cp', 'D', 'E', 'nocap')}
+# interaction: does the effect of removing the queue term / arrivals differ between the signals?
+for name, (x, y) in {'queue': ('B', 'A'), 'arrivals': ('Cp', 'A'), 'both': ('D', 'A')}.items():
+    a, b = C(x, 'random'), C(y, 'random')
+    c, d = C(x, 'score'), C(y, 'score')
+    dd = {k: ((a[k]['total_drops'] - b[k]['total_drops']) - (c[k]['total_drops'] - d[k]['total_drops'])) * 100 / E for k in a}
+    S['effects'][f'interaction {name} (random minus score)'] = dict(mean=round(float(np.mean(list(dd.values()))), 2),
+                                                                  ci95=boot(dd), ci95_cluster=boot(dd, cluster=True))
+json.dump(S, open(os.path.join(R, 'rev5_cap_summary.json'), 'w'), indent=1)
+print(f'ECMP mean drops {E:.0f}')
+for k, o in S['cells'].items():
+    print(f"{k:14s} total {o['total_pct']:6.2f}% fab {o['fabric_pct']:6.2f}% host {o['host_pct']:5.2f}% del/t {o['delivered_per_tick']:7.1f} "
+          f"mean {o['mean_ms']:.2f} p99 {o['p99_ms']:.2f} rr {o['reroutes']:7.1f} reord {o['reordered_pct']:.2f}%  {o.get('vs_ecmp', '')}")
+for g, e in S['effects'].items():
+    if isinstance(e, dict) and 'mean' in e:
+        print(g, e); continue
+    print('--', g)
+    for k, v in e.items():
+        print(f'   {k:36s} {v}')

+"""Concern 2 (fourth review): paired comparisons of the FlowBender-style baseline with score-without-rules and with
+the guarded schemes on the benchmark seeds 3000-3009 (60 runs each; existing results, no new runs).
+Inputs: rev4_fb.jsonl (fb_default, open loop), rev2_noguard.jsonl (score_no_rules), instrument.jsonl (ECMP and guarded;
+guarded GNN / LSTM there use one training seed). Differences: FlowBender minus other; drops in points of the mean ECMP
+total drops; paired bootstrap, 2,000 draws, seed 42. -> results/rev5_fb_compare.json"""
+import json, os
+import numpy as np
+R = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))), 'results')
+L = lambda f: [json.loads(l) for l in open(os.path.join(R, f))]
+fb = {(r['family'], r['seed']): r for r in L('rev4_fb.jsonl') if r['id'].startswith('open|fb_default|')}
+nr = {(r['family'], r['seed']): r for r in L('rev2_noguard.jsonl') if r['variant'] == 'score_no_rules'}
+ins = L('instrument.jsonl')
+g = {v: {(r['family'], r['seed']): r for r in ins if r['variant'] == v}
+     for v in ('ecmp', 'guarded_score', 'guarded_persistence', 'guarded_gnn', 'guarded_lstm')}
+assert len(fb) == len(nr) == 60 and all(len(v) == 60 for v in g.values())
+E = np.mean([r['total_drops'] for r in g['ecmp'].values()])
+
+
+def boot(d):
+    v = np.array([d[k] for k in sorted(d)]); rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
+    return np.percentile(v[rng.integers(0, len(v), (2000, len(v)))].mean(1), [2.5, 97.5]).tolist()
+
+
+def cmp(a, b, f):
+    d = {k: f(a[k]) - f(b[k]) for k in a}
+    return dict(mean=float(np.mean(list(d.values()))), ci95=boot(d),
+                fb_lower=int(sum(x < 0 for x in d.values())), ties=int(sum(x == 0 for x in d.values())))
+
+
+M = {'total_drops_pp': lambda r: r['total_drops'] * 100 / E, 'delivered_per_tick': lambda r: r['total_delivered'] / 150,
+     'mean_ms': lambda r: r['delivered_mean_latency_us'] / 1000, 'p99_ms': lambda r: r['delivered_p99_latency_us'] / 1000,
+     'reroutes': lambda r: r['reroutes']}
+S = dict(ecmp_mean_drops=float(E), fb_vs={})
+for name, other in [('score_no_rules', nr)] + [(v, g[v]) for v in ('guarded_score', 'guarded_persistence', 'guarded_gnn', 'guarded_lstm')]:
+    S['fb_vs'][name] = {m: cmp(fb, other, f) for m, f in M.items()}
+json.dump(S, open(os.path.join(R, 'rev5_fb_compare.json'), 'w'), indent=1)
+for n, o in S['fb_vs'].items():
+    print(n, {m: (round(x['mean'], 2), [round(c, 2) for c in x['ci95']]) for m, x in o.items()})
